SLAC scientists find conclusive proof of a mysterious third type of magnetism

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

For nearly 90 years, physicists have divided magnetic materials into two main categories: ferromagnets and antiferromagnets. Now, scientists have confirmed that a third type exists, revealing a previously unrecognized form of magnetic behavior that could lead to major advances in computing and electronics.

The breakthrough comes from a research team led by the U.S. Department of Energy's SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory. In a study published in Science, the researchers presented compelling experimental evidence for altermagnetism, a distinct magnetic phase that combines important characteristics of the two traditionally recognized types.

"In the past few years, scientists have observed phenomena that don't fit neatly into the ferromagnetism-antiferromagnetism dichotomy," explained lead author Daniel Jost, associate scientist at SLAC and Stanford Institute for Materials and Energy Sciences. "Researchers have been compiling evidence of a third type of magnetism that bridges the qualities of these two classes. We believe our results provide robust experimental confirmation that this third phase -- altermagnetism -- is real."

The team focused on manganese telluride (MnTe), a material that had long been classified as an antiferromagnet. Building on earlier research, the scientists identified direct evidence of chirality, a distinctive asymmetry in the material's magnetic structure. This property resembles the way a spiral staircase or screw twists in a particular direction and provides a crucial signature of altermagnetism.

Beyond its importance for fundamental physics, the finding could help researchers develop magnetic materials with properties suited to future technologies, including advanced memory systems, quantum devices, and more energy-efficient data centers.

Understanding the Two Traditional Types of Magnetism

Magnetism is familiar from everyday experiences, whether it is a refrigerator magnet holding up a photograph or a compass aligning with Earth's magnetic field. But the physics behind these effects is far more complicated than it appears.

At the atomic level, magnetism is connected to a quantum property of electrons known as spin. Although spin is not literally an electron rotating like a tiny ball, it behaves in certain ways as though the electron has its own angular momentum. Scientists commonly describe the two possible spin orientations as "up" and "down."

When these spins become organized into particular patterns, a material can exhibit magnetic properties. However, that does not necessarily mean it will produce an externally detectable magnetic field.

In ferromagnetic materials, electron spins align in the same direction (up-up-up or down-down-down). Their combined effect produces the familiar magnetic field associated with ordinary magnets. People have used materials with this behavior for thousands of years.

A second category, identified in 1933, works differently. In antiferromagnets, neighboring electron spins point in opposite directions (up-down-up-down). Because the magnetic contributions oppose one another, they cancel out, leaving no overall magnetization.

A Third Type of Magnetism Challenges the Old Rules

For almost nine decades, these two categories provided the basic framework for understanding how magnetic order arises in materials. However, experimental observations in recent years began revealing behavior that neither category could fully explain.

Some materials displayed alternating electron spins and no overall magnetization, just like antiferromagnets. Yet they could also support useful spin currents, a property associated with ferromagnetic behavior. Spin currents allow information to be carried through the spin properties of electrons rather than relying entirely on their electrical charge.

The unusual combination suggested that scientists were dealing with something fundamentally different. In 2022, researchers introduced the term "altermagnets" for materials belonging to this proposed third magnetic class.

The key difference lies in the arrangement of atoms within the material. Altermagnets have alternating spin orientations (up-down-up-down), but the crystal structures surrounding the oppositely aligned spins are rotated relative to one another.

This rotation breaks a form of symmetry that exists in conventional antiferromagnets. Although the distinction is subtle, it can dramatically change how electrons behave, allowing altermagnets to combine properties once considered incompatible.

Scientists Find the Missing Evidence for Altermagnetism

Even as experimental support for altermagnetism accumulated, researchers still needed a decisive way to establish that it represented a genuinely distinct magnetic phase.

"By 2024, there were some factors that spoke strongly in favor of altermagnetism being real," Jost said, "but there was still a missing piece to the puzzle."

That missing evidence involved chirality, a property sometimes described as "handedness." An object is chiral when its mirror image cannot be perfectly aligned with the original. Human hands provide a simple example: Your left and right hands are mirror images, but a left hand cannot fit properly into a right-handed glove.

In an altermagnet, the unusual arrangement of atoms and electron spins can create this kind of handedness in the material's electronic and magnetic structure. Conventional antiferromagnets do not exhibit the same characteristic chiral signature.

Detecting that difference would provide a powerful way to distinguish a true altermagnet from a material that merely resembles one.

The researchers selected manganese telluride as their test material. For decades, scientists considered it an ordinary antiferromagnet, but more recent studies had raised the possibility that it belonged to the newly proposed category.

Powerful X-Rays Reveal a Hidden Magnetic Signature

To investigate the material's unusual properties, the team conducted experiments at the European Synchrotron Radiation Facility (ESRF) in France. The facility's ID32 beamline produces precisely controlled polarized X-rays, allowing scientists to examine subtle magnetic structures that are otherwise extremely difficult to detect.

The researchers used circularly polarized X-rays, which have electric fields that rotate in either a left-handed or right-handed direction as the light travels. By measuring how the material scattered these two forms of light, they could search for evidence of chirality.

"Chiral materials scatter left-handed polarized X-ray beams differently than they do right-handed beams," explained Jost. "Non-chiral materials would scatter each beam of light the same way."

The experiment revealed a clear difference in the way manganese telluride interacted with the two X-ray polarizations. This distinctive chiral response was precisely the kind of signal expected from altermagnetism and would not be present in a conventional antiferromagnet.

"This chiral signature gave us the solid experimental ground to say altermagnetism stands in a class of its own," Jost said.

The researchers first performed the experiment in 2024. They returned in early 2025 and repeated the measurements, successfully reproducing the results and strengthening the evidence for the new magnetic phase.

"This technique helped us confirm that manganese telluride was really exhibiting altermagnetic properties," said Rachel Nickel, postdoctoral scientist with ESRF, a co-author who helped design and conduct the experiment on the ID32 beamline. "Altermagnetism was only proposed a few years ago, and the rapid progress in confirming these models is incredible."

Why Altermagnetism Could Transform Future Computers

The discovery could have important consequences for spintronics, a growing field that seeks to use electron spin to develop more capable electronic devices.

Conventional electronics store and process information primarily by controlling the electrical charge of electrons. Spintronics adds another way to work with information by taking advantage of electron spin as well.

This extra capability could enable devices that operate faster, withstand interference more effectively, and consume substantially less electricity. Such improvements would be especially valuable in computer memory, large data centers, and other technologies that require enormous amounts of information processing.

However, the two traditional types of magnetism present different obstacles that have limited the development of spintronic systems.

"Ferromagnets are too easily influenced by magnetic fields," Jost explained. "If you have a permanent magnet in your pocket and walk by a spintronic device built with ferromagnets, you could start flipping the bits, scrambling the information stored there. Antiferromagnets fix that problem, but they are practically invisible -- so you can't easily read the data stored in them or use them to drive efficient spin currents."

Altermagnets offer a potentially valuable alternative. Like antiferromagnets, their opposing spins produce no overall magnetization, helping protect them against unwanted magnetic interference. At the same time, their unusual electronic structure allows them to generate spin-polarized currents that can be used to retrieve information.

"Altermagnets combine the best of both," Jost said. "They have zero net magnetization, so they're stable, but they still have the capability to generate spin-polarized currents needed to easily extract information. That gives you an additional degree of freedom to work with."

A New Frontier for Quantum Technology and Electronics

Confirming altermagnetism gives scientists a new class of materials to investigate and potentially engineer for technological applications. By combining magnetic stability with the ability to manipulate spin-based information, these materials could help overcome challenges that have held back conventional spintronic devices.

The next step will be learning how to control altermagnetic states using external influences such as light and electricity. Researchers hope that mastering this control will make it possible to design practical devices that take advantage of the unusual magnetic properties.

Although significant development is still needed before these materials can be widely used, the findings establish a stronger experimental foundation for future work on quantum technologies, computer memory, and more efficient data centers.

The research received partial funding from the DOE Office of Science.

NASA Announces X-70 Experimental Aircraft to Advance Flight Research

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

On Wednesday, NASA unveiled its newest experimental aircraft, the X-70 Fractal, during the UP.Summit at the agency’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida. This high-performance, highly adaptable research platform is designed to accelerate the development and testing of advanced aviation technologies.

The crewed X-70 features advanced, rapidly reconfigurable flight-control technology that will allow NASA to explore aircraft maneuverability in ways never before demonstrated in crewed flight. The aircraft can also accommodate up to 2,000 pounds of experimental payloads in internal and external locations, enabling a wide range of research and flight-testing opportunities.

“The X-70 Fractal gives NASA a crewed, high-performance platform to test advanced flight-control, maneuverability, and other cutting-edge technologies in real flight,” said NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman. “Its modular design and ability to rapidly swap internal and external payloads will keep our teams flying, learning, and moving quickly from one experiment to the next. We expect her first flight under the NASA banner within the next two years, and X-70 is just one part of this new beginning for aeronautics. This is why people come to NASA: to build, to learn, and most importantly, to fly.”

The X-70 Fractal has a takeoff weight of 10,000 pounds and is designed to cruise at Mach .75, reach altitudes of up to 45,000 feet, and provide an operational range exceeding about 1,726 miles (1,500 nautical miles). These capabilities will enable NASA to conduct demanding flight research across a broad range of operating conditions.

“For decades, NASA Aeronautics has used X-planes to develop technologies that advance aviation,” said Laurie Grindle, director of the Aeronautics Division within NASA’s Research and Technology Mission Directorate. “It’s exciting to bring on another X-plane to accelerate and drive aeronautics research.”

The X-70 joins a storied legacy of experimental aircraft that have helped establish America’s leadership in aviation. From the Bell X-1, which first broke the sound barrier in 1947, to the X-15 hypersonic research aircraft and today’s X-59 quiet supersonic demonstrator, NASA has spent decades pushing the boundaries of flight.

With the X-70, NASA will expand its capacity to conduct experimental flight research, accelerate the testing of emerging technologies, and advance the next generation of American aviation.

For more information about NASA’s aeronautics research, visit:

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

Securing the agentic enterprise with data lineage

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Data lineage is the new security perimeter, providing the visibility needed for governance, accountability, and trust, without impeding AI adoption at scale.

A sales representative asks an AI agent for customer pricing, and the agent pulls data from multiple sources: restricted pricing systems, internal communications, and archived quotes. The output looks accurate to the agent, so a second agent uses the data to draft the proposal for the sales rep. No human checks either handoff. When it’s discovered that the proposal includes unapproved, confidential, and sensitive information, no one can trace where the problematic data entered the workflow or how it spread across systems, files, and agents.

This scenario illustrates why AI governance needs to evolve, rapidly and dynamically. As more enterprises begin to use agentic AI in daily workflows, they’re realizing that they must manage more than static files; they must manage moving and fragmented data that is continuously ingested, copied, summarized, transformed, reused, and redistributed in new forms. In short, AI security isn’t a tool problem; it’s a data visibility problem.

Consequently, traditional security approaches built solely around applications, systems, networks, or endpoints are no longer sufficient. Securing agentic AI at scale requires understanding how data moves. Teams, therefore, need to establish data lineage in order to effectively manage governance, accountability, and trust across human, agentic, and hybrid workflows.

The Download: blended meat, restored sight, and the agentic web

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Plus: Democrats have dealt tech billionaires a major midterm setback.

This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology.

In the fall of 2025, Jeff Dodge served hungry students steaming marinara sauce and meatballs. The students loved them: 92% of those who tried them wanted them on the menu. Few could guess that mushrooms had been mixed into the meat.

Dodge was testing a new product called 50/Cut from the company Joyn. The blend of seemingly simple ingredients—available as meatballs, burgers, and ground meat—could help solve one of the biggest challenges of the climate crisis: getting people to eat less meat, particularly beef.

Joyn is among a wave of startups creating “blended meat,” which combines plant proteins with the real deal. The logic: The best meat replacement is … better meat.

Get a taste of the startups betting on greener beef.

More than 5 million people have an advanced form of age-related macular degeneration (AMD) called geographic atrophy, in which retinal cells die off. Until now, no treatment could restore their vision. A system called PRIMA changes that.

It combines a retinal implant with camera-equipped eyeglasses to improve sight in patients who develop a blind spot in the middle of their vision. After training sessions, more than 80% of recipients could read, do crosswords, and use their phones.

Find out how the sight-restoring implant works.

In 2001, a decade after inventing the World Wide Web, Tim Berners-Lee articulated a new vision: an internet navigated by autonomous software agents that address our every need, without requiring us to muck about with browsers and search engines ourselves.

At the time, the idea seemed far-fetched. But a quarter-century on, AI agents are beginning to make it a reality. They can triage your inbox, do your grocery shopping, and even access your bank accounts.

This new era is being called the agentic web, and it will inevitably transform the internet. Whether the human web survives this transition is anyone’s guess.

Democrats look to reshape America after historic midterm victories

Party promises dramatic change in the wake of its ‘blue wave’

What’s next for US energy policy after the midterm elections?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Don’t expect huge reversals right away, but we could see more support for renewables.

I’ve been locked in to my various screens since results from midterm elections started to roll in last night. And it didn’t take long to see the beginning of the blue wave: Democrats took control of both the US House of Representatives and the Senate, a dramatic swing from Donald Trump’s victory two years ago.

While the results are striking, we probably won’t be seeing some of the big climate and energy policy shifts that have come in recent years as Congress and the presidency have changed hands. Instead, we’re entering a period of divided government, with a Congress that will likely do battle with Trump’s White House over the next two years. But even if the federal government isn’t likely to pass laws that overhaul energy and climate policy, the midterm results could be a big deal for energy, especially at the state and local level.

Let’s step back for just a moment to put these results in context. Readers might remember huge laws affecting climate and energy that were passed in just the last four years. The 2022 Inflation Reduction Act included massive support for clean energy, including tax credits and government grants and loans.Then the 2025 One Big Beautiful Bill Act essentially slashed much of the support and financial incentives in the IRA from two years before.

What those two laws had in common was a period when the government was entirely united, with the Senate, House of Representatives, and presidency all controlled by the same party—the Democrats in the first case and the Republicans in the second

Now Democrats have captured a majority in both the Senate and the House. But Donald Trump is still in the White House. And crucially, neither chamber is anywhere close to a two-thirds Democratic majority. That’s the ratio that would be needed to override a presidential veto.

So even if Congress puts together new legislation to boost climate measures and clean energy, Trump will need to sign it—and that dramatically narrows the scope of what might happen legislatively between now and the next election.

There could be some bipartisan compromises. One interesting bit of legislation to watch immediately after the election is what happens with permitting reform. In late September, a bipartisan group of senators put forward a bill that would overhaul how new energy projects are reviewed and approved in the US. The law aims to cut red tape and speed up the permitting process.

The Senate is set to vote on that package, called the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, on November 9, before the new Congress takes control in January. The White House has supported the bill, and it could pass the Senate if just a handful of Democrats join the Republican majority. But now, we might see Democrats wait until next year to try to renegotiate a deal, though anything would still need to pass muster with Trump.

With Democratic control of Congress, we’ll likely see much more scrutiny of Trump and agencies controlled by his appointees. Democratic leaders have already signaled plans to go after leaders like EPA commissioner Lee Zeldin over cuts to staff and investments. Democrats will have the power to subpoena cabinet members and open investigations.

Looking beyond the national results, a major trend to watch moving forward will be local action. Democrats swept local control of Pennsylvania, and officials there have signaled plans to support renewable energy and efforts to cut greenhouse-gas emissions. Democrats also took control of the Georgia Public Service Commission for the first time in 30 years. The group regulates electric utilities, essentially controlling much of the grid’s buildout.

Among the broadest effects of these election results, though, will be for the data center industry. According to tracking from Ballotpedia, there were 41 local ballot questions related to data centers. And overwhelmingly, voters chose to restrict their development. Nearly every single one of these measures looks set to come down against data center development, as of the morning of November 4.

And AI critics fared well in their races, too. Democrat Gina Hinojosa won the governor’s seat in Texas, ousting Republican incumbent Greg Abbott. During her campaign, Hinojosa called for a law to completely pause data center development; if passed, it could have major implications for both the state’s grid and the data center industry more broadly.

We might not see dramatic new climate- or energy-related federal legislation between now and the next major US election. But many voters signaled, and quite loudly, that they wanted a change. We’ll see how much will shift in the next two years, and where that leaves us.

Texas was the new epicenter of the AI data center buildout. That might be kaput.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Some of the largest AI infrastructure projects in the world are underway in Texas. A dramatic political shift could slow their construction—and deepen concerns about the economics of the AI boom.

Texas is the fastest-growing data center market in the US and ground zero for Big Tech's largest AI buildouts. But the blue wave that swept the Lone Star State may jeopardize that position.

Democrat James Talarico won the US Senate seat against Ken Paxton. Gina Hinojosa won the race for governor against the Republican incumbent, Greg Abbott. At the state level, Democrats will take control of the Texas House for the first time in 24 years. Talarico and Hinojosa made data centers a central issue, discussing them constantly on the campaign trail.

Even before the polls closed, insiders in the data center industry noticed that the times were changing. At the industry's DCD conference in Ashburn, Virginia, on Tuesday, panelists lamented that Texas had been the easiest place to build for years—until September. That's when Abbott, perhaps seeing the increasingly bipartisan distaste for these facilities, began changing course himself.

Developers said the governor’s office had stopped responding to their emails. On September 21, Abbott directed a key state regulator to halt data center permits. Many panelists at the conference were expecting this to mean that existing projects might be slowed and new ones could be halted altogether.

The governor-elect indeed seems eager to do that. Hinojosa supported tax breaks for data centers in 2023 as a way to boost construction jobs, but during her campaign she began pushing for a full-fledged moratorium.

That would be a problem for the hyperscalers of Big Tech, which have ambitious plans for AI training facilities in the state. The scale of projects already underway in Texas is massive. The Stargate Frontier data center in Shackelford County is projected to serve 1,820 megawatts—about as much as the nation’s largest data center, SpaceX’s Colossus 2 in Tennessee, and more than twice as much as the largest data center currently operating in Texas. Nexus Hubbard, also under construction and intended mostly to serve Anthropic, has a planned size of 6,400 megawatts and is the second-largest data center being built in the US, behind only Meta’s Hyperion project in Louisiana.

The potential for Democrats to order delays to these projects or moratoriums on new ones adds to growing anxiety that the AI buildout could stall. Banks and lenders are already getting pickier about the projects they back. And given the extent to which the data center buildout is underpinning growth in the economy right now, those forces combined could have ripple effects.

Voters around the country, though, were unambiguous in the midterms, sending a similar message on everything from state ballot questions to national offices. Voters supported efforts to protect ratepayers from increases in their electricity bills caused by data centers, and they favored some larger bans on new data centers altogether. If we needed any more convincing that Big Tech has fumbled its effort to sell voters on the idea that AI-powered abundance will make health care better, goods cheaper, and scientific breakthroughs more common, this was it.

A key question is whether the Texas counties with the largest data center presence were part of the reason these Democrats got elected, or if the anti-data-center vote came from elsewhere.

It’s complicated: The counties where much of the data center buildout is happening are around Dallas. Dallas County and others closest to it predictably went blue, but nearby Ellis County, which leads in permitting requests for data centers, went for Greg Abbott and Ken Paxton. Cameron County, near the state’s southern border, is a hot spot for data centers and voted blue in the midterms, which was a shift. But Mitchell County and Scurry County, where a bunch of projects in west Texas are located, went red.

This is a big deal, because no single office in the state decides whether a project goes ahead or is halted. Decisions often reflect an interplay between city governments, county officials, and state offices.

Local officials who have some say over data center development may resist sweeping restrictions from the state. And Democrats pushing for a moratorium might also face pushback from the construction unions that supported some of their campaigns. So while the blue wave spells a death knell for Texas data centers in theory, election winners may find that following through on their opposition once in office is much more complicated than campaigning on it.

Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible.

An exclusive interview with Nick Cammarata, the researcher whose 26,000-word viral blog post has AI safety folks celebrating.

Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny.

Surveillance towers, increasingly powered by AI, promised faster responses to crossings. Our investigation of deaths near the towers found they are failing to deliver.

Winners, losers, the EU: Five takeaways from the US midterms

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Donald Trump's Republicans suffered a crushing defeat in Tuesday's midterm elections, with the Democrats wresting control of Congress. What does this mean for US politics and the world?

The Democrats not only made significant gains in Tuesday's midterm elections but also took control of both chambers of Congress. Here are five insights that can be drawn from this moment.

The storming of the Capitol, attempts to change the rules of the electoral process, a spate of presidential executive orders to bypass Congress and many more examples sparked concerns that the US' vaunted democracy was being transformed into an authoritarian system under Donald Trump. But the country's democratic institutions have proven more resilient than many had feared.

Many voters were driven by their opposition to Trump's desire to rule with unfettered power. Instead they want to see country's institutional safeguards strengthened.

A  post-election survey by news agency Associated Press found that about 30% of voters thought the state of democracy was the country's biggest problem. About 70% felt that Congress was not doing enough to provide checks and balances on the president.

This midterm result sends a clear signal that voters want stronger parliamentary oversight. President Trump could of course continue trying to govern through executive orders, bypassing Congress. But Congress sets the budget.

This means the Democrats, who now control Congress, can deny the president the funds he needs for his political initiatives. They can also set up investigative committees, which puts the Trump government under considerable pressure to justify its actions.

For a long time, the Democratic Party lacked compelling leadership, a unified approach and a clear answer as to how it intends to win votes. Tuesday's election proves the party is still a political force to be reckoned with.

Its gains in many Republican strongholds are nothing short of sensational. The party was even able to score victories in Texas and South Carolina. In Kansas, the Democrats won a Senate seat for the first time since 1932.

The midterms have also put the spotlight on a new generation of Democrats: Younger politicians, some of them noticeably more progressive, who could chart a fresh course for the party.

Take, for instance, the Democratic Socialists of America, or DSA, an organization which is considerably more left-wing than the Democratic Party itself. At least five candidates endorsed by the DSA have been elected to the House of Representatives.

The DSA advocate for strong welfare policies that focus on affordable housing, health care and workers' rights. They also want to see greater checks on the power of large corporations.

"The argument was always that these folks can't win on a national stage," Brandon Bohrn, an expert on trans-Atlantic relations at the Bertelsmann Foundation, told DW. Now some of them have defied the odds and actually won, potentially sparking an internal debate about what the Democrats stand for and how they plan to win voters in the 2028 presidential election, Bohrn said.

Donald Trump has already made it clear that he does not hold himself responsible for this midterms defeat, taking to Truth Social to deflect any blame.

Yet not everyone in the Republican party is likely to agree. Critics of Trump who had been sidelined may feel emboldened to speak up again. The question as to whether the Republicans should abandon Trumpism will be almost impossible to ignore.

By law, Donald Trump cannot run for a third term in the 2028 presidential race so Republicans will have to think about who can. But who will take the lead? And more importantly: On what policy platform?

Tuesday's election defeat will likely accelerate this debate, making a power struggle over the Republicans' political future inevitable.

The war against Iran and resulting economic hardships have cost the Republicans many votes. The cost of living, in particular, was a key issue for many American voters.

The consequences of the Iran war have primarily driven up energy prices. This is likely to significantly increase domestic pressure on Trump to reach a lasting agreement with Iran.

Trump does seem keen to cut foreign policy costs, especially in areas where it has been politically controversial for his party. Shortly before the midterms, he announced that he no longer intends to provide billions in military aid to Israel. This was in relation to a deal that was meant to expire in two years anyway.

Ian Lesser, an expert on US foreign and security policy at the German Marshall Fund, believes that the midterm elections could also impact trans-Atlantic relations. "This actually makes a big difference for Europe in a number of different important ways," Lesser told DW.

The Democrats could try to reinvigorate dialogue between Europe and the US, Lesser suggests. After all, with control of Congress, the Democrats have more say on Trump's NATO policy, can influence budget decisions and military funding, and can complicate planned cuts to US support. Europe and the US may even cooperate on climate and environmental issues.

"Simply restoring a degree of trust and predictability, however we get to that, makes a big difference," Lesser said.

Not all differences will be resolved overnight though.

"Tariffs are a little tricky because there are clearly vested interests in the US in keeping some of this in place," Lesser concluded. "But in terms of new and very aggressive tariff measures, I think Congress would absolutely push back against that, on both sides of the aisle, actually."

This article was originally published in German.

Bavarian politician Hubert Aiwanger found dead in Berlin hotel

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Aiwanger was Bavaria's Economy Minister and the chairman of the right-wing populist Free Voters party. The circumstances surrounding his death are still unclear.

Bavarian politician Hubert Aiwanger was found dead in a hotel in Berlin on Wednesday, the German capital's public prosecutor's office said.

Aiwanger was Bavaria's deputy premier, economy minister and the chairman of the right-wing populist Free Voters party. He was 55 years old.

The circumstances surrounding Aiwanger's death are still unclear. Police and prosecutors in the German capital have launched an investigation.

On Wednesday evening, investigators said the goal is to "determine the specific cause of death and the circumstances surrounding it, as well as to rule out any possible foul play."

Aiwanger had been feeling unwell on Wednesday, German tabloid Bild reported.

He was scheduled to deliver a speech at the opening of a party office in Berlin but canceled his appearance for health reasons, German news agency dpa reported.

Aiwanger's body was found on Wednesday evening at the "Neuer Fritz" Hotel on Friedrichstrasse in Berlin-Mitte, according to Bild.

Bavarian state premier, the Christian Social Union's Markus Söder, has said he is "deeply shocked" by the news of his deputy's death.

"The sudden death of Hubert Aiwanger is an absolute tragedy," Söder said in a post on X.

"Our thoughts are now first and foremost with his family and his children. We mourn with them and pray for them."

Born in Lower Bavaria in southern Germany, Aiwanger was elected to the Bavarian State Parliament in 2008 with the Free Voters and became their parliamentary group leader.

Since 2018, his party has been in a coalition with the center-right CSU, the Bavarian sister party of the ruling Christian Democratic Union (CDU). Aiwanger served as the state's Minister of Economic Affairs and deputy premier.

Aiwanger was the face of the Free Voters in Bavaria and across Germany, and was known for his combative approach.

But his political success in the state did not ranslate to the national level, as the Free Voters never won enough seats to enter the Bundestag, Germany's federal parliament.

In the weeks before state elections in 2023, Aiwanger became embroiled in a national scandal when the Süddeutsche Zeitung reported that he had written an antisemitic leaflet during high school in the 1980s, which included references to Nazi-era atrocities such as Auschwitz.

He is survived by two children. He was not married but lived with Tanja Schweiger, a district administrator for the Free Voters.

Democrats take House and Senate from Republicans in US midterm rout

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Democrats will control both chambers of Congress after flipping seats in Republican strongholds across the US in an election seen as a repudiation of President Donald Trump.

By late Tuesday night, it was clear that Democrats had succeeded in turning the 2026 US midterm elections into the so-called "blue wave" that had been predicted by pollsters.

But by Wednesday morning, the scale of the victory had turned into a "blue tsunami," surprising even the most optimistic political pundits.

In January, Democrats are set to have a commanding majority of at least 237 to 139 in the House and a 54 to 44 advantage in the Senate.

Democratic candidates flipped seven highly competitive Senate seats, including in what have for decades been reliably Republican districts in "red" states.

Texas voted in James Talarico as its first Democratic Senator since 1988, while electing a Democratic governor, Gina Hinojosa, for the first time in over 30 years.

The heartland state of Kansas, where farmers have been hit by high diesel prices and tariffs, will send a Democrat, Adam Hamilton, to the US Senate for the first time in over 90 years.

Other surprise wins in Republican strongholds included Iowa, South Carolina, and Ohio.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

According to data analyzed by the New York Times, the 2026 Democrats came in at a 13 point margin of victory over the Republicans for the popular vote in the House, the largest such margin since 1976 seen in the wake of the Watergate scandal that shattered the presidency of Republican Richard Nixon two years prior.

Democrats also saw large gains in almost every voter group compared with the 2024 presidential election that put Trump in the White House, indicating the breadth of the political swing away from Republicans.

The full scale of the Democrat majority in both chambers may not be known until later Wednesday or Thursday as some races are still too close to call, or take longer to count ballots.

The midterms were seen as a referendum on President Donald Trump, who had historically low approval ratings of around 38% ahead of election day.

Trump had campaigned relentlessly in the weeks leading up to the election, in what often turned into meandering speeches to dwindling crowds.

Democrats were able to capitalize on Trump's unpopular policies like the war in Iran and ensuing record-high fuel costs, which compounded a growing affordability crisis while the president was seen as enriching himself.

On Wednesday, Democratic House leader Hakeem Jeffries said his party was focusing "relentlessly on affordability and on accountability."

"The days of the House of Representatives functioning like a wholly-owned subsidiary of the Trump administration are over," said Jeffries, who could become the first Black speaker of the House in January.

Democrats say they are ready to launch a 100-day agenda to lower costs for fuel, groceries and healthcare, while conducting vast oversight and investigations into Trump and his administration.

When asked about the option of impeaching Trump or cabinet members for breaking laws while in office, Jeffries said Democrats were not focusing on that for now.

The margin of victory in the Senate is still not a two-thirds supermajority, with which Democrats alone could vote to convict in cases of impeachment, or override presidential vetoes.

Even as a lame duck president for the remaining two years of his term, Trump still has the power of the executive at his disposal, which comes with broad sway, especially in foreign policy.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Despite for months claiming that Democrats would cheat to win the election, Trump did not dispute the Republican blowout. Instead, he claimed that Republicans would have won had his name been on the ballot.

Nevertheless, Trump said he would be willing to work with Democrats, while at the same time promised to use veto power to block legislation.

In remarks on Wednesday outside of his ballroom construction site at the White House, Trump appeared to admit that the economic impacts of the Iran war had cost the Republican Party.

"I think it's largely true because it drove the gasoline up a little bit short term," Trump said.

Earlier Wednesday, Vice President JD Vance was more direct, saying the Iran war was the "reason we lost."

However, Trump spent the majority of the time in front of reporters on Wednesday talking about his ballroom project.

"It's going to be not only one of the biggest, but it'll be the most beautiful ballroom I think anywhere in the world," he said.

NASA's Swift space telescope plunges back to Earth in fiery demise

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

NASA's Swift orbital observatory fell back to Earth after running its mission for more than two decades. A rescue attempt to raise its sinking orbit had earlier failed.

A premier NASA space telescope met a fiery end as it crashed back to Earth —  two decades after its launch —  following the failure of a rescue mission.

The unmanned Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory re-entered the Earth's atmosphere over the North Sumatra region of Indonesia on Wednesday at 7.39 am EST (12.39 pm UTC), the US space agency said.

Most of the spacecraft was expected to have burnt up as it traveled through the atmosphere, NASAsaid, adding that some components may have survived re-entry.

NASA's prized Swift observatory — equipped with three telescopes — was launched into orbit in 2004.

The observatory was tasked with studying gamma ray bursts, the biggest ​energy blasts in universe for two years. But its mission spanned 11 times longer than planned, lasting just a few days short of 22 years.

The $500 million (€444 million) Swift satellite weighed roughly 1,452 kilograms (3,200 pounds).

It studied hundreds of supernova explosions, star-shredding black holes, stellar X-ray flares, asteroids and ​comets.

Swift also contributed to nearly 7,000 published scientific ⁠papers.

The satellite, which was in a good condition, fell out of orbit partly due to powerful geomagnetic storms set off by solar eruptions.

In July, NASA roped in private company Katalyst Space and launched a mission to raise the altitude of the Swift observatory.

But Katalyst Space's three-armed robotic servicing spacecraft — called LINK — faced issues with attitude control soon after reaching space, causing it to spin uncontrollably.

The rescue vehicle plunged back to Earth in September, while Swift ceased operations on October 20.

There were no immediate reports of damage on ground as the Swift observatory fell back to its home planet.

Typhoons batter East Asia as El Niño makes Pacific 'burn with fever'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Fishermen return to sea under a crimson sky in August after China's Jiangsu province is hit by Super Typhoon Dolphin

Wild weather is a way of life for people living along the coast in the Philippines, Taiwan, China and Japan. But the last few months have been extraordinary.

A wave of intense and, at times, unpredictable storms exacerbated by the El NiÃ±o weather pattern has killed dozens, shuttered major airports and wiped out crops and infrastructure across a swathe of East Asia.

In October, nearly all of Taiwan shut down as a monster storm big enough to engulf the island of 23 million roared towards its north-eastern coast. Equivalent to a category five hurricane, Typhoon Champi's 295km/h (193mph) winds had the power to flatten homes and whip up destructive waves.

Thankfully it did not make landfall in Taiwan, skirting past the island while dousing it in rain. But it had far-reaching impacts: hundreds of flights were cancelled in Hong Kong, Taiwan and Japan. In the Philippines' Batanes archipelago, seaside highways shut as huge waves crashed onto the shore, and in southern Japan's Okinawa islands, power was cut to 15,000 homes.

El NiÃ±o is a naturally occurring climate phenomenon in which warm waters pooling in the eastern Pacific and altered wind patterns drive extreme weather events around the world.

It happens around every two to seven years, lasting for around a year. This year's El NiÃ±o, which the UK Met Office has forecast to be the "strongest in living memory", is likely to last until February or March.

In June, Japan watched two storms - Typhoon Mekkhala and Severe Tropical Storm Higos - race towards its eastern coast simultaneously, forcing one million people to evacuate. In July, Tropical Storm Narra grazed China's southern coast along Guangxi, submerging villages and displacing tens of thousands.

This riverside park in the south-eastern Chinese city of Nanning was submerged by Tropical Storm Narra

August brought Super Typhoon Dolphin, which churned across the Pacific for 16 days - before slamming into eastern China, triggering heavy floods and dozens of casualties.

Later that month Typhoon Saudel made landfall in three south-eastern Chinese cities over one week, shutting transport networks and forcing thousands to evacuate.

The Pacific Ocean, heated to record levels by El NiÃ±o, is now brimming with typhoon energy, according to the Accumulated Cyclone Energy (ACE) metric which measures the total intensity of a season's storms. This year, the ACE reading for South East and East Asia is forecast to be nearly double the 30-year average.

El NiÃ±o also affects wind patterns and in this case, that means typhoons are tending to form further east than usual. They are also becoming stronger as they remain over warm waters for longer.

The Pacific can be likened to someone "burning with fever", says Ana Liza Solis, head of climatology at the Philippines' national weather bureau. "It can go into convulsions soon," she said at a press briefing in Manila in September.

Forecasts suggest the worst may not be over. Last month, sea surface temperatures in the equatorial Pacific - the area monitored by the Japan Meteorological Agency for its El NiÃ±o predictions - soared to 4C above normal, the highest since records began in 1949. The previous record - 3.6C - was set in December 1997.

China's climate agency has warned of sea surface temperatures continuing to rise over the next three months because of the El NiÃ±o.

Despite typhoon season usually peaking around September to October, this is likely to raise concerns across a region that has already been battered by back-to-back storms.

China has seen back-to-back typhoons as the 2026 El NiÃ±o intensifies

In August, Typhoon Chan-Hom baffled storm watchers in Japan by crossing the country from east to west, as opposed to the usual south-to-north trajectory. Japan's well-resourced and prepared authorities were able to evacuate people swiftly, but this would be far more challenging in some neighbouring countries.

To get a better picture of how rising temperatures could amplify the risks, Manila-based climatologist Rafaela Jane Delfino and colleagues simulated the effects of warmer conditions on the Philippines' most recent monster storms, including 2013's Super Typhoon Haiyan (Yolanda) which killed more than 6,000 people.

They arrived at what she describes as "very, very scary findings": higher temperatures increased total accumulated rainfall by 26%. It's a devastating prospect in a country where flooding from monsoon rains and some 20 tropical storms per year has emerged as one of the main disaster risks.

In recent years, floods have paralysed entire cities for days, and tired of the annual ordeal, Filipinos have taken to the streets to protest against corruption which they say is gnawing away at millions of dollars from flood-control projects.

Delfino, in an article on the University of the Philippines website, insists leaders and officials must gain a better understanding of climate hazards if communities are to be better prepared.

Tide of rubbish blankets Philippines river in August after weeks of monsoon rains

Countries are bracing for the immediate impact. Japan has urged citizens to stockpile food and essentials for the typhoon season, and to secure outdoor furniture that could become deadly projectiles when typhoon winds hit. China has asked local officials to identify vulnerable areas and is upgrading its warning and monitoring systems.

The Philippines and its South East Asian neighbours are preparing for disruptions to food production, especially rice, and have increased support for famers, including the provision of fertilisers.

But what happens in the long term is less clear. The window for governments to act on El NiÃ±o is "closing", the United Nations Development Programme in Asia and the Pacific said recently.

"How severely a community experiences an El NiÃ±o is decided years in advance, by where roads and settlements are built, how water is managed, who is covered by social protection and whether public budgets have room to absorb a shock.

"Every El NiÃ±o tests the resilience we have built; this one should also drive us to build more," it added.

Responsible or panicked? Meet the people around the world already preparing for crisis

French blood storage impacted by freezer hacks as theories swirl about culprits

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

French authorities say that fridges used in medical facilities to store blood for donations were impacted by recent cyber-attacks by unknown hackers.

The French Blood Establishment (EFS) said cold rooms storing blood were impacted by hackers, with temperatures set to rise - but staff intervened to protect blood supplies.

"No health impact has been identified and no loss of blood products is to be reported," the EFS said.

Up until now, only supermarkets were known to have been impacted by the hacks.

Meanwhile rumours have been circulating online about Russian hacktivist groups claiming they worked together to hack one of the systems that was used in the attacks which have affected European supermarkets.

Noname057(16) and Z-Pentest Alliance posted that they successfully hacked Danfoss which led to speculation that they were the culprits behind the freezer hacks.

But analysts now question whether or not that meant they were behind the supermarket cyber-attacks.

While Russian groups are not being ruled out, experts say Iranian hacktivists or nation state hackers - or even teenagers pulling pranks - could also be the culprits.

Cyber-security researchers at multiple firms are poring over evidence gathered from so-called "honeypots" to provide clues as to who is behind the waves of freezer hacks.

Three separate cyber-security firms have deployed fake freezer software traps to monitor how hackers attempt to use them for attacks.

In cyber-security, a "honeypot" is a digital decoy - such as a fake computer network or file - deliberately set up to look like a vulnerable target to lure and trap hackers.

By monitoring these traps, security experts can safely study an attacker's methods without putting real systems at risk.

According to activity gathered after the first wave of cyber-attacks, the second wave which hit several European countries was highly targeted.

"The fact that all these fridge freezers were hit simultaneously suggests planning and coordination," says Ryan Dewhurst from Spanish cyber-security company Previdian.

The entry point to the affected freezer software products was not difficult to exploit as they were poorly protected, with known attack methods already floating around online.

But experts like Dewhurst say the hacker or hackers had to gather the information about the devices' public IP addresses to create a target list then take time to plan which methods to use and when to execute them.

"Given the reported timing of when the devices had their temperatures increased, which seems to have been overnight for maximum damage - this is further evidence that the attack was planned and co-ordinated," he said.

Another cyber firm which has deployed similar honeypot tech to lure the hackers into revealing their tactics is Strathclyde-based Lupovis.

It had honeypots which appeared to be located around the world, and started seeing activity 10 days before the second wave of cyber-attacks.

Intriguingly, the team saw hackers scanning and probing their fake freezer sites only in Europe.

"We saw none of our honeypots in places like Africa or South America targeted at all," said Lupovis founder Dr Xavier Bellekens.

A third company - French cyber-defence firm Sekoia - also noticed scanning activity prior to the second wave of cyber-attacks, but said it could have been automatic scanning without malicious intent.

In a report to its customers, it said evidence suggested the nature of the attack aligned with activity of Russian hacktivists or the Russian government, which is being accused of carrying out so-called "hybrid warfare" cyber-attacks across Europe.

But Sekoia's research also points to Iranian regime hackers or hacktivists too.

"Iranian-aligned actors have an established record of targeting internet-exposed industrial control systems and previous incidents involved the same equipment in Israel and the US - both adversaries of Iran," the report said.

According to Sekoia, about 40 Israeli supermarkets running Danfoss controllers were compromised back in 2022.

Meanwhile in late August, freezers in at least six US military bases were forced into defrost mode, as in France. The controller models remain unconfirmed and investigations are ongoing.

"To date, we have no technical evidence indicating the involvement of Russian-speaking groups," a Sekoia spokesperson told the BBC.

"Furthermore, as NoName057 is not known to effectively conduct operations beyond DDoS attacks and has a history of making false attributions, we remain sceptical of their alleged claim of responsibility."

Distributed denial of service (DDoS) attacks work by flooding a website or online service with internet traffic in an attempt to throw it offline, or otherwise make it inaccessible.

Authorities in several countries are actively investigating the hacks and warning supermarkets about potential future attacks.

None of the three cyber firms which have been gathering honey pot data are ruling out that it could be a teenager.

But with the degree of planning necessary the experts all agree it would have to be a well co-ordinated group - and if so, they would expect online bragging to surface soon.

Sekoia also said the possibility remains that all three waves could have been carried out by different and distinct groups or copycats.

If hackers can take control of a supermarket freezer, how vulnerable is other tech?

Shop freezers in seven European countries hit by cyber-attack

Joshua Wong, face of Hong Kong's pro-democracy protests, jailed for 10 years

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The 30-year-old was the face of Hong Kong's now-quashed democracy movement

Joshua Wong, once the face of Hong Kong's democracy movement, has been sentenced to 10 years in jail for colluding with foreign forces under the city's sweeping national security law.

Wong is among hundreds of campaigners and ordinary Hongkongers arrested under the law, which was imposed in 2020 following explosive pro-democracy protests against Beijing's growing control.

Critics say the far-reaching law has silenced dissent, but authorities argue that it is needed to maintain stability and deny it has weakened the city's cherished autonomy.

Having already spent the last six years in jail, Wong, who was due to be freed in January, will now spend another decade behind bars.

Silenced and erased, Hong Kong's decade of protest is now a defiant memory

What a defiant mogul's trial says about Hong Kong

Wong, who turned 30 last month, has already been imprisoned multiple times for his activism which started when he was a teen.

On Thursday, he arrived in court wearing a dark blue long-sleeved shirt, khaki trousers and black-rimmed glasses. He looked slimmer compared to his previous public appearances.

He nodded in greeting towards members of the packed public gallery, and took notes intermittently as the judge read out the reasons for his sentencing.

A crowd had gathered outside the court, with dozens of Hongkongers having queued for hours hoping to get a seat in the public gallery. There was also a heavy police presence.

Wong is already serving time for subversion and was due to be freed early next year when he was handed the latest charges, which he has pleaded guilty to earlier in September.

Two months and two days of the 10-year term will be served concurrently with his ongoing sentence, the judge said on Thursday.

Prosecutors said that Wong had conspired with other campaigners, including self-exiled activist Nathan Law, to lobby for foreign sanctions and blockades against Beijing.

These activities took place between July and late November 2020, the court heard.

Wong and Law had been co-founders of Demosisto, a now defunct pro-democracy political party.

Wong's friend and fellow activist Frances Hui, now based in the US, says she is "heartbroken" and "outraged" by the verdict.

"Today's sentencing is not just about Joshua. It is part of a broader political crackdown that seeks to rewrite a chapter of Hong Kong's history and erase the contributions of an entire generation who believed they could shape the future of their city," she told the BBC.

Human Rights Watch described the ruling as "cruel and unjust".

"To keep Hong Kong society subdued, and to reduce the chance of an international outcry, the Hong Kong government appears to have adopted the tactic of stacking charges against influential pro-democracy leaders," said its Asia director Elaine Pearson.

The harshest sentence under the controversial national security law (NSL) thus far has been handed to former media tycoon Jimmy Lai, who was jailed for 20 years, also for colluding with foreign forces.

Wong's (left) activism started when he was just 14

Born to IT professional Roger and homemaker Grace in October 1996, Wong was diagnosed with dyslexia as a child, which made him struggle with school.

However, he was quick to challenge established rules and norms when they didn't make sense. In secondary school, he rallied with his classmates to protest against the high prices of canteen lunches on Facebook.

In 2011, Wong, then 14, co-founded the pro-democracy student activist group Scholarism, which quickly grew in size and influence.

The following year, the group rallied more than 100,000 people to protest against Hong Kong's plans to implement mandatory "patriotic education" in schools, forcing the authorities to back down.

At 30, Wong has already been imprisoned multiple times for his activism

By 2014, Wong, who was then at the forefront of a civil disobedience movement known as the "Umbrella Movement", had already become one of the most recognisable faces of Hong Kong's pro-democracy movement.

In 2016, he co-founded Demosisto with Law and Agnes Chow, an activist who is now also in exile.

But he often expressed discomfort at being labelled a "protest leader".

"We have a lot of facilitators in this movement and I'm one of them...âInstead of having a top-down command, we now have a bottom-up command hub," Wong told The Financial Times in November 2019.

Demosisto disbanded in 2020, the year Beijing introduced the NSL in Hong Kong.

Law left for the UK and continued his advocacy abroad, while Wong stayed on in Hong Kong and tried to run for a seat in its legislature â but was barred, with authorities citing his opposition to the NSL as one of the reasons.

Meanwhile, the court cases against Wong were stacking up.

On 23 November 2020, he pleaded guilty to several charges related to his involvement in protests outside the Hong Kong police headquarters in 2019.

Then came the NSL charges. While still in prison in February 2021, Wong was among 47 people charged with "conspiracy to commit subversion". The group became known as the Hong Kong 47 and remains the largest prosecution so far under the NSL.

Wong was still serving his 56-month sentence for the subversion conviction when he was charged with collusion under the NSL last year.

On 22 November 2020, the day before he pleaded guilty to the protest charges, Wong wrote in a post on Instagram addressed to "the people of Hong Kong": "To be honest, I have never had much faith in Hong Kong's politics, but I have every confidence in the people of Hong Kong.

"Even as political power now poses a comprehensive challenge to the resilience of our civil society, I believe the people of Hong Kong will, as always, find a way to see things through."

What Democrats can and can't do with their new power after taking Congress

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Democrats 'got their wish': Voters react to midterm results

Election night was a fever dream for Democrats. In January, reality will set in.

They had a reason to be giddy on Tuesday night: Next year, Democrats will take control of both chambers of Congress after a blowout victory.

"Today begins the great American comeback," an ecstatic Hakeem Jeffries, House Democratic leader, declared.

After months of promises on the campaign trail â to investigate corruption; lower the costs of healthcare, groceries and petrol; and curtail the Iran war â they will now begin the much tougher process of putting plans into action.

A shift in the balance of power in Washington doesn't necessarily mean Democrats will immediately get to start checking items off their legislative wish list. Democrats have Congress, but Republican President Donald Trump still has the power to reject legislation.

The Democrats should not consider what just happened "a mandate," US Senator Chris Coons told the BBC, warning that the party was still "deeply unpopular".

"We need to prove we can get stuff done, actually deliver answers to their concerns and questions about low wages and rising costs in particular."

James Talarico just flipped Texas' US Senate seat to the Democrats, helping them win control of Congress' upper chamber.

Congressman Robert Garcia of California will soon lead a powerful oversight committee in the House, with the power to make Trump's last two years in office quite difficult.

"Lawyer up," the Democrat said in a statement as victorious results poured in on election night.

Committees in both the US Senate and House have investigative powers, which they could use to delve into alleged conflicts of interest and other actions by the president and those close to him. On Tuesday, Garcia rattled off a list of possible targets: Trump's family, "corporate corruption", alleged abuses by the Department of Homeland Security and Immigration and Customs Enforcement, and the government's handling of the Epstein files.

The Senate also has the power to confirm or block the president's cabinet and judicial nominations.

Should a US Supreme Court vacancy, for example, arise during Trump's term, Senate Democrats could use their new power to block a nomination - or force Trump to choose a more moderate candidate who garners bipartisan support.

How voter shifts in Texas helped elect a Democratic senator and governor

The House also has the power to impeach a president - the first step of Congress' process to remove an official from office.

Democrats twice impeached Trump during his first term, though the US Senate eventually voted to acquit him in both cases. But because it takes a two-thirds majority in the US Senate â or 67 votes â convicting a president in an impeachment case likely will remain out of reach for Democrats.

There's an active debate among Democrats about whether impeachment is a good idea. The first impeachment, in 2019, unleashed partisan rancor and allowed Trump to cast himself later as a victim of political persecution, which later helped build support for his second White House run.

Jeffries, who would almost certainly have to greenlight any Democratic impeachment effort, has kept his plans vague.

"Well, we've made clear: We haven't ruled anything in," he told CNN in August. "And we haven't ruled anything out".

Americans' frustration with Congress' productivity was palpable before the election. Only 14% of US registered voters approved of lawmakers' performance, an October Gallup poll suggested.

But with a Republican in the White House and Democrats running Congress, Americans may face even more deadlock.

"If you thought legislating was hard in the last Congress with unified Republican control, that will get much more complicated," said Casey Burgat, professor of legislative affairs at George Washington University.

Because they will control both chambers with a comfortable majority, Democrats will be able to pass legislation through Congress with relative ease.

Getting it enacted into law will be harder.

A president must sign most legislation in order for it to become law. Trump has the power to reject legislation passed by Congress that he does not support, and Congress can only override him with a supermajority from both chambers - margins Democrats alone will not have despite their success on Tuesday.

As his party lost Senate race after Senate race Tuesday night, Trump struck a conciliatory tone with the New York Times.

"We'll work with the Democrats, and we'll, you know, look forward to working with them. We'll probably work something out," he said.

But a few minutes later, Trump posted a picture on Truth Social of his red campaign hat with the word "VETO" emblazoned across the front. That Jekyll-and-Hyde approach to a Democratic Congress could become a theme.

While opposition to Trump led to Democrats' success, lawmakers acknowledged Tuesday night, it might not be enough.

"It is overwhelmingly a rejection of Donald Trump, and his agenda and policies," Coons told the BBC.

"But if we overreach, or if we fight too much internally, we could miss this moment."

Hakeem Jeffries, leader of US House Democrats, greets party staffers on election night.

Despite the veto threat, Jeffries has said that his party wants to restore significant cuts Republicans made to healthcare programmes like Medicaid, which is used by low-income groups, and the Obamacare marketplace.

"We're committed as a top priority as House democrats to reversing these Medicaid cuts and extending the Affordable Care Act tax credits," Jeffries said at a September event for healthcare workers.

On typically bipartisan issues â such as policies affecting affordability, regulating the AI industry, re-evaluating military funding for Israel and reining in the Iran War â talks could break down as lawmakers try to compromise. As they become the opposition heading into the 2028 presidential cycle, Republicans might not be in the mood to help Democrats achieve too many wins.

Fissures also could erupt even among Democrats themselves, particularly on matters involving Israel, that may make party consensus difficult. A schism emerged in the Democratic Party during the midterms over continued US military funds for Israel.

Incoming senators like Abdul El-Sayed of Michigan, and some incoming members of the US House, said during the campaign they would vote for legislation to curtail aid to Israel. These Democrats could face a road block from their own leadership.

Then there's the matter of reining in Trump's foreign policy, which could be difficult, despite Democrats' majority.

Even before the midterms, Congress saw bipartisan efforts to limit Trump's ability to continue the war with Iran. Though some of these measures passed one or both chambers, Congress has not been able to enforce them. Notably, both the House and the Senate passed a resolution in June to halt hostilities with Iran, which Trump could not veto but has essentially ignored.

The White House has argued Trump has broad powers to continue the war. But Congress' authority to force Trump's hand is an "open legal question," said Scott Anderson, senior fellow in governance studies at the Brookings Institution.

"Congress can try to litigate this out, and force the courts to tell us."

Vance walks Trump tightrope with scathing election critique blaming Iran war

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

With Congress flipped, what does Donald Trump do next?

Vice-President JD Vance sent shockwaves through Washington on Wednesday by blaming his party's midterm defeat squarely on the Iran war, in what was widely interpreted as an effort to distance himself from President Donald Trump ahead of a potential 2028 White House run.

In a carefully calibrated, 600-word statement on X, the vice-president acknowledged that Trump's 2024 voters had deserted Republicans in the midterm election.

"The reason we lost is the war," he wrote. "The president himself said so last night."

In interviews late Tuesday night and Wednesday, Trump had defended the war but acknowledged that it played a factor in Democrats flipping control of the US House of Representatives and the Senate.

Vance's view on the war in Iran is an open secret in Washington. While he has repeatedly backed the conflict since the US and Israel attacked Tehran in February, he has reportedly expressed opposition in private meetings with administration officials.

But those disagreements had been kept out of the public eye - until now.

Could Democrats in Congress end the war in Iran?

Hours after Trump and the party were handed a bruising election defeat, Vance made clear that he felt the decision to go to war was a clear political mistake.

"If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so. Argue it was a worthwhile cost to pay. Agree or disagree, that's at least honest," Vance said.

It was a striking statement, and one with no recent precedent: it's hard to recall a similar moment in recent US political history when a sitting vice-president has so sharply broken with the president on a signature foreign policy issue.

Then came an arguably even greater surprise: Trump - who typically demands steadfast loyalty from his senior advisers - agreed with Vance that the midterms were "largely" decided by the war. He added that he would "do it again" if given the same choices.

BOWEN: Trump's loss limits powers at home, but it will likely be different abroad

THE BIG MOMENTS: Ecstatic scenes that greeted key results

ZURCHER: Midterm carnage weakens Trump but he still wields power for now

'A DISASTER': Angry Republicans blame Trump for midterms defeat

It was an early sign that Trump - who has prized loyalty from his advisers - may give Vance a wide berth should he choose to launch a presidential campaign.

"I know how he feels about it. I think it was -- I think it was fine," Trump told reporters when asked about Vance's post blaming the war. "I knew that when I went in, and we stopped them from having a nuclear weapon, and they will never have a nuclear weapon."

Vance's statement seemed like "a way of separating himself from Trump," said Daniel Byman, a foreign policy expert at the Center for Strategic and International Studies.

Drawing contrasts with Trump will be a delicate balancing act if Vance seeks the presidency, Byman added.

That balance was on display in Vance's post. The vice president didn't mention Trump by name and attributed much of his election results analysis to unnamed pollsters.

Vance "put his toe in the water to see what he's going to do," Ashley Davis, a Republican strategist, told the BBC.

Beyond the Iran war criticism, Vance also offered a political roadmap that could preview a possible presidential campaign. He distilled the party's strategy to win back voters into a neat four-point gameplan: "1) End the war 2) Focus on cost of living/inflation 3) Put America First 4) Change our relationship to Israel."

The list encapsulated much of Vance's worldview since bursting onto the national political scene, and read like the core argument in a future stump speech.

The BBC has contacted Vance's office for comment.

Of course, the next presidential election is still two years away. Vance is expected to run but hasn't made an official decision, and aides insist that he's still mulling it over.

It's also up for debate exactly how much Vance broke from Trump. Vance showed the message to Trump before posting it online, according to The New York Times.

Trump, for his part, could change his mind and make clear he'll brook no dissent from Vance or anyone else.

The president has been circumspect about who he'd support to succeed him as the 2028 Republican presidential nominee. Trump has praised Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio on several occasions, but hasn't said when - or if â he'd make an endorsement.

The president appears to relish the guessing game of who he'd back to carry on his political movement, even in the past floating the idea that he'd continue to a third term (which is barred by the US Constitution).

Vance's midterms missive might not be that significant in the end. Or it could have been the starting gun for the 2028 race and the battle for the post-Trump Republican Party.

'This is a disaster': Angry Republicans blame Trump for midterms defeat
