Russia, China may be more aggressive if they feel Trump is on 'his way out': Brookings

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Russia and China could become more aggressive over the next two years if they conclude that President Donald Trump is politically weakened and on "his way out" after Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress, Brookings Institution senior fellow Robert Kagan told CNBC.

Speaking on "Squawk Box Asia," Kagan said the war with Iran had also exposed how Washington has "proved itself rather incapable militarily" in terms of capacity, which would be critical in Asia.

"Everybody can see how much our stocks are depleted in terms of interceptors and missiles, which are of vital interest in the Asian theater," he said.

Congressional Budget Office reports have estimated that the conflict depleted as much as two-thirds of U.S. missile-defense interceptors since June 2025, largely because of efforts to defend Israel against Iranian attacks. This could leave the U.S. with constrained inventories for several years.

Kagan said the deployment of three U.S. aircraft carriers to the Middle East had reduced the U.S. naval presence available in Asia, potentially giving China more room to act assertively in the region.

"I think Trump's only interest right now is in cutting economic deals with China. I don't think he has any real interest in confronting China, but that is going to lead China to be even more aggressive," he said.

In December, Trump approved an $11 billion arms package for Taiwan, but later held back on a second package worth about $14 billion, calling it a "bargaining chip" in talks with China.

China claims self-governing Taiwan as its territory. While Washington does not have a mutual defense treaty with the island, U.S. law requires it to provide Taiwan with the means to defend itself.

Former CIA Director William Burns said in 2023 that Chinese President Xi Jinping had instructed the military to be ready to invade Taiwan by 2027, while stressing that this did not mean Xi had decided to launch an invasion that year or at all.

Kagan's comments come as U.S., China and Russia discuss a possible trilateral summit between Trump, Xi and President Vladimir Putin at the APEC Leaders' meeting in Shenzhen later this month.

Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson Mao Ning said Wednesday that Beijing was "in communication with relevant parties regarding a meeting between the three heads of state," although no agenda has been announced.

Putin had reportedly floated the idea of a three-way summit with Trump and Xi in October to discuss ending the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.

"If it's clear that Donald Trump is on his way out, and the United States may be returning to something like normal, I don't want to overstate that, but they may feel both of them, both Russia and China, may feel that the time to move is in the next two years," Kagan said.

Can the $179 Fitbit Edge beat the $249 Apple Watch SE 3?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I recently tested the fitness tracking performance of the Fitbit Edge vs. Apple Watch 12, and much to my surprise, the more affordable Fitbit came out on top against one of the best smartwatches in 2026.

But can the Fitbit Edge pull a repeat win against the Apple Watch SE 3? It is, after all, a more direct competitor to the Edge, both in terms of price and features, compared to the Series 12.

To find out, I walked exactly 5,005 steps (manually counted) with the Fitbit Edge logging my efforts from my left wrist and the Apple Watch SE 3 recording the workout from my right wrist. As a control for distance, climb, and pace, I also recorded the walk in the Strava app on my iPhone 16 Plus.

You can find the results of my Fitbit Edge versus Apple Watch SE 3 walk test below, along with the five biggest takeaways from the data, including an overall winner declared at the end.

But first, here's a quick rundown of the key differences between these two popular wearables. The Apple Watch SE 3 starts at $249, is iOS-only, and balances impressive health and fitness features with smart features galore.

The Fitbit Edge starts at $179, works with Android and iOS devices, and also boasts comprehensive holistic and workout-logging chops, but few smart features. That said, the more petite Fitbit Edge gets roughly a week of battery compared to about a day for the Apple Watch SE 3.

When it comes to logging a basic walking workout, both devices are evenly matched with onboard GPS and barometric altimeters for precise location and elevation tracking, along with continuous heart rate monitoring.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

The Fitbit Edge logged 5,016 steps during my roughly 45-minute walk, just eleven steps off from my actual total. The Apple Watch SE 3 didn't do too shabby either, logging 109 step fewer than my manual count.

As always, my rule of thumb for these tests is that any step count within roughly 100 steps of my actual total is considered excellent and well within a reasonable margin for error. While the SE 3 is just a hair outside this threshold, this is still a solid result for the entry-level Apple Watch.

One of the biggest differences between the Fitbit Edge and its predecessor, the Fitbit Charge 6, is that the newer model has an altimeter for logging climb data; the older model doesn't. More importantly, the Edge logs uphill efforts with proven accuracy, from my testing, and this demo only adds to that conclusion.

The Apple Watch SE 3, meanwhile, inflated my climb by a similar margin to the Apple Watch 12 in my previous walk test versus the Edge.

The Apple Watch SE 3 (worn on my right wrist) logged higher heart rate data than the Fitbit Edge; however, the Fitbit Edge calculated more calories burned during the walk.

Could this discrepancy in heart rate be a result of the devices tracking my workout from different wrists? Probably not. After all, the best Apple Watches and best fitness trackers, including the Edge, are designed to be worn on the wrist of your choice.

That said, it's up to you to set your chosen wrist preference in the settings/companion app, as I did for this test, to ensure the best possible data.

The Fitbit Edge has the Apple Watch SE 3 easily beat when it comes to overall battery performance; the former lasts up to a week per charge compared to 18-24 hours per charge for the latter.

However, when it comes to GPS battery life, the two are apparently quite evenly matched. Both devices burned through 6% battery during my ~45-minute walk, an outcome I wasn't expecting.

Ultimately, the Fitbit Edge outperformed the Apple Watch SE 3 in this 5,005-step comparison of fitness tracking performance.

The Edge calculated nearly the same amount of steps taken as the manual tally and nearly identical elevation gain data too. That said, the SE 3 was a tiny bit closer to the control distance-wise.

However, even with the Edge coming out on top, I'd still happily use either of these modern wearables as my go-to health and fitness tracker, which begs the question: which do you prefer, the Apple Watch SE 3 or the Fitbit Edge? Let me know in the poll below.

As Social Media Shoots for Long-Form, Should Brands Invest in Creator TV?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

As we’ve grown accustomed to in the age of social media trend cycles, Instagram rolled out yet another update last week. But rather than the usual micro-adjustments, the platform’s announcement of Features — allowing creators to upload horizontal videos of up to 90 minutes — heralds a more radical shift. After years of establishing the dominance of short-form-everything and laying waste to traditional broadcast channels, social media platforms are now planning for a future built around bona fide entertainment: programming across long-form and medium-form, serialized, and genre-agnostic formats, spearheaded by creators and adopted by brands. Welcome to the age of creator TV.

A transformation from social media to entertainment media is intensifying. TikTok’s rewards and subscription benefits now favor longer-form videos. Substack is encouraging its essayists to produce live video events. YouTube has launched Stations, letting creators run always-on channels for rolling longer-form content. Viewing functionality is changing, too: Instagram is becoming smart-TV compatible (more than half of YouTube watch-time in the UK now takes place on TVs).

But why are social media platforms readily modifying their tried-and-tested playbook? Netizens’ appetites for diets made up exclusively of get-ready-with-me (GRWM) content, dance routines, and brainrot ‘comedy’ are weakening. Scrolling exhaustion, influencer antipathy, and performativity pressures are real: over half of Americans say maintaining a social media presence ‘feels like work’. Audiences, struggling with context collapse and algorithmic anxiety, want depth. They’re looking to rebuild frazzled attention spans, retreat from disposable content, and re-engage with knowledge and storytelling — creating a clear opportunity for fashion brands to retool their lore, heritage, and creative nous into richer entertainment.

Max Cocking and Louby Mcloughlin, co-founders of social-first digital content agency Dislo, which has worked with Ferragamo and Fendi, describe this as the juncture where brands need to acknowledge “clip fatigue” and dare to encourage “more mindful and thoughtful consumption”. “You can buy engagement, you can buy attention, but you can’t buy resonance. This is what Instagram has realized: you can’t get that from clips anymore. Longer-term storytelling is what builds relationships and authority,” says Cocking. “Brands should be commissioning series, not posts,” Mcloughlin adds.

The smartest content creators are already “flipping their original ideas into social-first TV”, says Mcloughlin. We’re in a purple patch for culturally credible ‘internet shows’: episodic social media productions synthesizing documentary, interview, and pop culture critique. Energized by plentiful memetic internet parlance, these shows are becoming standalone legitimate, admired entertainment franchises.

On-the-rise examples include Noah Blau’s Errands, a lo-fi docu-series elevating everyday mundanities into mini-odysseys; Jack Coyne’s Track Star, a music-based interview show sitting at the top of the tastemaxxing pyramid; comedian Kareem Rahma’s Subway Takes, handing the mic to guests sharing ‘unpopular opinions’; and Revival of the Fittest, a slow, surreal paean to a stalwart record store. Brands are already starting to borrow these shows’ watchability and cultural cachet by backing (rather than hijacking) their formats: for instance, dating app Feeld sponsors episodes of Interior Motives, a queer-coded show that critiques people’s bedroom decor.

Creator Jack Coyne filming music quiz Track Star with guest Niall Horan.

According to Chelsie Hares, writer and researcher at trends intelligence business Stylus, “creator-led entertainment pairs the allure of social media’s relatable figures with storytelling that is worth viewers’ time investment. As audiences worry about the cognitive toll of unfulfilling content, these shows offer the familiarity of the feed, but take people beyond the same-old scroll towards the higher caliber of TV.”

Fashion trend analyst and brand consultant J’Nae Phillips sees these shows as an evolution of the creator model towards something more sustainable. “The distinction between ‘creator’ and ‘entertainment producer’ is collapsing as the most ambitious creators are building formats, franchises and production worlds rather than simply posting endlessly interchangeable content,” they say. For brands looking to these burgeoning franchises as they reconsider their marketing strategies, they offer an invaluable blueprint for moving from “interrupting entertainment to helping create it”.

Creator and podcaster Ben Mora, Courtney Love, and Ivy Wolk on an episode of internet ‘gameshow’ Interior Motives.

Developments like Instagram Features represent attempts to keep this activity within social media environments, retaining both audience attention and the brands that build visibility from it. In the US, 52% of consumers (73% among Gen Z) say social media is their main way to discover new entertainment, but 44% (60% of Gen Z) say they leave the platforms to consume it in full, per Forbes. Social media platforms don’t want to be a discovery funnel — they want to be an entertainment destination and cultural stage.

While the early-2020s script of peak influencer marketing is wearing thin for many, the era of creator TV is inspiring a refresh for consumer-brand relations. In North America, 66% of 13 to 29-year-olds say they’re interested in brands creating original films or content series in place of traditional advertising, according to YPulse. Consumers are inviting brands to echo creators’ new direction as cultural arbiters and creative forces, “thinking less in terms of campaigns and individual assets”, says Phillips, “and more about building formats and franchises that can generate cultural equity over time”, and letting the hard sell take a back seat. Lucinda Bounsall, founder of culture-led social strategy agency Sibling Studio, agrees: “Brands don’t become meaningful through isolated moments of visibility. They become meaningful by creating things people recognize, connect with and want to return to.”

These new possibilities will provide particularly fertile ground for fashion marketing, “because the best fashion brands already understand the value of building worlds and narratives, acting like cultural publishers rather than conventional advertisers”, explains Phillips. As brands rush to join the global microdrama boom, many of the most successful branded versions have come from fashion, including Crocs, Marc Jacobs and Banana Republic. Elsewhere, fashion marketing has been experimenting with social media-native storytelling formats for some time: Alexis Bittar, founder of the eponymous jewelry and accessories brand, is a pioneer in using serialized, fictional content in place of traditional marketing via ongoing mockumentary series Bittarverse (launched in 2023) and 2026 true-crime parody The Sexecutions of Amanda Gates. In these shows, fictionalized characters live out sensational micro-dramas wearing the label.

“I was finding traditional marketing completely uninspiring,” says Bittar. “I wanted to wake people up, to tell stories that were more complex and nuanced. I didn’t want to do the hard sell; I wanted to keep the viewer interested in a narrative. And that builds community; storytelling helps people relate, build interest, and keep returning.” And it’s paid off, generating robust brand equity and attracting new consumers to the brand, he says. “It’s allowed me to create a more fully formed ethos and world around the brand. Much of luxury fashion is about making people want to be part of an elite club, but I wanted more inclusivity, more complexity. The result is real loyalty to the brand.”

Characters in jewellery brand Alexis Bittar’s New York mock reality social media series The Bittarverse.

This is also a moment to reconsider social media’s mass-appeal imperative. “Success doesn’t necessarily have to mean mass attention. There’s an enormous opportunity for brands to create something smaller and more meaningful to a particular audience,” says Bounsall. In the worlds of fashion, design, and culture, the brands willing to pursue niche appeal are taking inspiration from creator-led internet shows’ tastemaker positioning, using their own cultural cachet to “tune into the more specific fandoms around their brand in a way that can scale in longer form”, says Cocking. Take StockX’s Listed, a new original video series inviting fashion collectors to show off their grail pieces, with showcased items shoppable on the StockX platform. Figma, meanwhile, has launched Source Material, a series featuring creatives and designers explaining their inspiration.

Stills from design platform Figma’s social media show Source Material, featuring creative director and creator, Chase Rutherford.

As the entertainment axis shifts, marketers’ options will diversify, not contract. Clips and short-form video will stick around, but increasingly as “trailers, discovery mechanisms and entry points into longer-form worlds”, says Phillips. Branded entertainment for social media’s TV age doesn’t always have to be super slick, either. “Sometimes the more polished something becomes, the more it starts to feel like an ad. The brand investment should be in the ideas and talent, not necessarily the production values. What entertainment needs most is a point of view,” says Bounsall.

A redrawn brand-creator continuum beckons, with more “creators owning their franchises”, says Cocking, with brands acting as backers rather than upfront advertisers. Further platform functionality updates from Instagram and YouTube will encourage more creators into serialized storytelling, paving the way for long-running, brand-backed creator TV living on socials, with major e-commerce and promotional potential. And even as influencer marketing’s effectiveness wanes, it’s crucial that brands don’t bypass the richness of a recalibrating creator community in their haste to produce their own entertainment, says Bounsall. “Fashion has a long history of nurturing creative communities, whether that’s photographers, musicians, artists or designers. Why shouldn’t the next generation of creator comedians, writers and entertainers be part of that?”

The It-Kids of the Philippines

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

It-Kids is a series in which Vogue Business spotlights the up-and-coming global talent brands need to know, in partnership with Launchmetrics and global Vogue editions.

During Fashion Month, Filipino talent proved to be the breakout stars.

The Philippines was the fourth-biggest market for celebrity media impact value (MIV) at Paris Fashion Week, surpassed only by Thai, South Korean, and Chinese talent, and beating out the US and Japan. Across 97 total posts, Filipino talent generated $9.8 million in MIV for the week.

Before Paris, at New York and Milan fashion weeks, Filipino talent also outperformed. Fashion industry darling Heart Evangelista, one of the Philippines’ buzziest stars (she started out as a child actor and is now an influencer and beauty founder), was one of MFW’s top five celebrity voices, generating $9.8 million in MIV, and was the only non-Thai celebrity in the week’s top three. Evangelista was also NYFW’s top celebrity voice, generating $7.6 million in MIV alone, accounting for roughly 17% of all celebrity MIV for the week. Tennis player Alex Eala was another NYFW hit after appearing at Calvin Klein.

It comes off a big summer for up-and-coming Filipino stars. Eala made an international splash and has already dipped her toes into the fashion world, attending Tory Burch and Calvin Klein fashion shows during New York Fashion Week. Late last month, the animated film Forgotten Island hit theaters, based on Filipino folklore and featuring Filipino and African-American talent H.E.R and Liza Soberano, and was met with overwhelmingly positive reviews — despite underperforming at the North American box office, thanks in part to steep competition from releases including Primetime and Avengers Endgame: Encore.

“The Philippines is producing a generation of talent that brands are only beginning to tap. Their audiences are loyal and deeply engaged, and the conversation around them travels well beyond their own posts,” says Launchmetrics CMO Alison Bringé.

Here’s a list of some of the fastest-growing Filipino names, based on their social media followings, content and reach, per Launchmetrics’s growth and Voice Echo analysis, as well as global Vogue editorial consultations.

Tennis player Alex Eala has had a big year. The 21-year-old has become a closely watched sportsperson since her breakthrough win at the 2022 US Open, when she won the girls’ singles title. Then, she became the Philippines’ first Grand Slam champion (and landed the cover of Vogue Philippines shortly after). Since then, she’s established herself as the highest-ranking Filipino and Southeast Asian in WTA Tour history. Throughout her rise, Eala has dabbled in fashion, and this season attended shows including Calvin Klein, Michael Kors, and Chanel. Expect to see her on the circuit in seasons to come — when she has a moment between Grand Slam matches.

Known for: Being the highest-ranking Filipino and Southeast Asian in WTA Tour history

Brand affiliations: Rhett Eala — $1.1 million in MIV ($310,000 from direct impact; $767,000 from indirect echo), Chanel — $2.6 million in MIV ($531,000 from direct impact; $2.1 million from indirect echo), Calvin Klein — $1.8 million in MIV ($454,000 from direct impact; $1.3 million from indirect echo)

Actor and singer Atasha Muhlach has just wrapped her role as Annie Batungbakal in the musical Bongga Ka, 'Day! in Manila. She also hosts the TV show Eat Bulaga!, which she began working on in 2023, the same year she released her first single. She’s been starring as Annie Batungbakal onstage since September, and recently featured in the series Da Pers Family and Bad Genius. Muhlach has appeared on the covers of Filipino editions of Tatler and L’Officiel.

Known for: Eat Bulaga!, Da Pers Family, Bad Genius

Brand affiliations: Bvlgari — $44,700 in MIV ($44,300 from direct impact; $469 from indirect echo), Bench — $183,000 in MIV ($20,000 from direct impact; $163,000 from indirect echo)

Dancer Niana Guerrero got her start on YouTube in 2017, where she became known for her dance videos, and swiftly moved to TikTok in late 2019. She now boasts over 40 million followers on the platform. Recent cameos in her dancing videos have included Icelandic singer Laufey and British singer Flowerovlove, with whom she performed onstage at Coachella in April. (Guerrero’s siblings often feature alongside her in her videos, too.)

Brand affiliations: Polo Ralph Lauren (May) — $513,000 in MIV ($513,000 from direct impact), Polo Ralph Lauren (July) — $360,000 in MIV ($360,000 from direct impact), Francis Libiran — $450,000 in MIV ($327,000 from direct impact; $123,000 from indirect echo)

Average engagement rate (Instagram): 1.09%

Actor Elijah Canlas is one of the Philippines’ fastest-rising young actors, after debuting in his first role — in Sundalong Kanin — at age 14. He’s starred in films including Edward and Kalel, 15, as well as the YouTube series Gameboys. He stars in the Edgar Jopson biopic Edjop, which delves into the life of the Filipino activist. Next, he’s set to feature in the forthcoming crime drama Jaguar, set in Manila, which will premiere in 2027.

Brand affiliations: Pentshoppe (Filipino brand) — $13,700 in MIV ($5,500 from direct impact; $8,200 from indirect echo), Nike — $3,100 in MIV ($3,100 from direct impact)

Filipino-Nepalese singer-songwriter Jason Dhakal was born in Oman and moved to the Philippines back in 2017. His debut album, Lovesound, was released in 2020, followed by his second, Being, in 2023. Dhakal’s third album, Escape!, was released in August, and he’s currently on tour around Manila. Earlier this year, Dhakal, 26, left his big label, where he was previously signed, to go independent. Escape! was released via indie music platform ONErpm Philippines.

Brand affiliations: Loewe and Victoria Beckham — $8,000 in MIV ($8,000 from direct impact), Comme des Garçons — $16,900 in MIV ($1,700 from direct impact; $15,200 from indirect echo)

Actor Gab Pangilinan got her start on-stage doing musical theater, which is still her primary work, though she’s emerged as a triple threat, working on-screen and in music as well. She starred in the web series Still in 2021, and her second film, Edjop, released in September. Pangilinan recently wrapped her role as Mary Magdalene in Jesus Christ Superstar in Manila, and soon, she’ll star in the stage production Gabriella.

Brand affiliations: ḢA.MÜ — $3,200 in MIV ($3,200 from direct impact), Sassa Jimenez — $1,900 in MIV ($1,900 from direct impact), Uniqlo x JW Anderson — $3,800 in MIV ($3,800 from direct impact)

Average engagement rate (Instagram): 1.2%

Santino Rosales is a soccer player and model, and currently plays for the Maharlika soccer team. He most recently featured in Rabanne’s Invictus Elixir fragrance campaign, and his Instagram feed is a mix of soccer, brand events, and food snaps, reflective of the balance of his two careers. Rosales recently featured on the cover of V Man Southeast Asia.

Known for: His work on the soccer pitch and in front of the camera

Brand affiliations: Rabanne — $1,700 in MIV ($1,700 from direct impact), Boss — $10,000 in MIV ($9,600 from direct impact; $477 from indirect echo), Dior — $4,900 in MIV ($4,900 from direct impact), Ferragamo — $3,800 in MIV ($3,800 from direct impact)

Filipino-Irish model Siobhan Moylan is based in New York now (for work), but grew up in the Philippines. Though she’s three years into living in the US, she kicked off the year with a L’Officiel Philippines cover, and is one to watch for major brands keen to tap fresh faces. She also makes TikToks recreating runway beauty looks.

Brand affiliations: Celine — $15,300 in MIV ($15,300 from indirect echo)

Painter Anna Bautista’s following is still relatively niche, but she’s fast gaining traction in the art world. Brands are catching on, too — she’s already worked with luxury houses like Tiffany & Co., as well as the likes of Levi’s and, most recently, Nike. She was the youngest artist in the group that her management, DF Art Agency, brought to Paris for its exhibition last month.

Known for: Her painting — across both art and fashion

Brand affiliations: Nike — $4,200 in MIV ($4,200 from direct impact)

These are the up-and-comers who are key for brands to keep an eye on as their stars continue to rise.

Kakie Pangilinan is a singer and actor who graduated from New York City’s The New School last year. She’s having a busy 2026, with the release of her latest single, Kontrabida, and her forthcoming role in Edjop alongside Canlas.

Don Baldosano is a 27-year-old chef who won the first Michelin Guide Young Chef Award in the Philippines last year. He currently runs Linamnam, a 10-seat restaurant in Manila.

Sisters Tasha and Bella Tanjutco are environmentalists who work with designers and artists to help invigorate their climate work, focusing on collaboration and creativity.

Sebastian “Basti” Escalante is a 28-year-old businessman who also works in the restaurant space. He’s working to bring his restaurateur father’s Antonio’s Group into its next era, with expansion into Metro Manila.

Gabby Padilla is an actor known for both her film and theatre work, including films Billie and Emma and Gitling.

Therese Malvar is a 26-year-old actor who has gained traction in recent years after getting her start in Ang Huling Cha-Cha ni Anita back when she was 13.

While influencer marketing once stopped at deliverables — how many posts or stories the talent would share, media impact value (MIV) now goes far beyond those static posts. “These ambassadors are gateways to PR coverage [and brands] need to 360-degree measure the direct and the indirect impact,” says Launchmetrics CMO Alison Bringé. “Additionally, there’s that layer of their own social media channels and how these campaigns are being engaged with by their own community.”

On average, when looking at celebrity-driven campaigns, 77% of MIV came from the indirect category, Bringé says. “It’s insane because there are still some very unsavvy brands that are just thinking about the deliverables and ‘how many posts can I get from you?’” she says. “They need to be thinking about how they can create virality instead.” Data gathered by Launchmetrics includes average MIV generated by placements with talent, including voice echo numbers for brands that the company tracks.

What Does the Blue Tsunami Mean for Fashion?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Democrats’ landslide victory in the midterm elections brings the global luxury and fashion sectors to a new juncture as the Democratic Party has flipped control of both the Senate and the House, midway through President Trump’s second term. While the “blue tsunami” has left the current administration downtrodden, the market responded with enthusiasm on Wednesday morning, with the Dow up more than 1,500 points — a 2.8% spike.

To mark the watershed moment, Hildy Kuryk, the interim CEO for the Council of Fashion Designers of America (CFDA) joined Nicole Phelps, global director for Vogue Runway and Vogue Business, on stage at the Vogue Business Global Summit: New York on Wednesday in a pop-up session to unpack the result’s potential impact on the global fashion and beauty industries.

The discussion addressed a string of obstacles brought in during the Republican party’s reign, including the 2025 tariffs that wreaked havoc on fashion’s supply chains, followed by the conflict in the Middle East. The latter has pushed up inflation, increased living costs, as well as gas and oil prices, all while heightening macroeconomic instability.

In the months leading up to the elections, consumer sentiment was shaky. In early October, 36% of registered voters cited inflation as the most important issue to them, according to a poll conducted by The Economist and YouGov. This was followed by jobs and the economy, at 11%. Sixty-nine percent of voters said they disapproved of Trump’s handling of inflation, and 44% believed that political conflict had spread too far into everyday life.

And yet, people have been shopping. In fact, many luxury conglomerates and brands — Hugo Boss, Kering, Zegna, and Hermès included — cited the US as bright spot in their latest quarterly earnings. “In particular, the Americas continued its double-digit trend, despite an increasingly demanding comparison base,” executive chair Ermenegildo “Gildo” Zegna said in a statement, noting the company’s 14% growth in the region. Hermès’s growth was led by the Americas, up 8.8% in the third quarter, and Kering’s only growth region was North America.

As sights turn to the 2028 presidential election, here’s what Kuryk said about making sense of the midterms.

Vogue: The CFDA Awards were just two nights ago. Congratulations.

Hildy Kuryk: Then I stayed up way too late last night watching the midterms.

Vogue: We woke up to news of a “blue tsunami”. What do the results tell us about where the US currently stands?

Well, the good news is the market was up this morning. I think that investors are reassured that there could be some calm coming. But I want to be careful. This was a blue wave, but it was not a progressive wave or a moderate wave. It was a Democratic wave — in all of its forms. We saw that Democrats know how to win in the cities. Republicans spent a lot of money redistricting — it didn't work. But Democrats learned how to win in rural areas, which I think is so important. This is a repudiation of the president and of the administration. Voters clearly wanted something different. We have to be careful because America is a 50-50 country, so these wild swings are not necessarily predictive of where the country is.

So many. We have the first black female governor, Keisha Lance Bottoms; the first Latina governor in Texas, Gina Hinojosa; the first indigenous woman elected governor in Deb Haaland in New Mexico; the first Muslim senator elected now to start in Michigan, Abdul El-Sayed; the first openly gay senator, Chris Pappas, in New Hampshire. Louisiana elected its first Republican woman, Julia Letlow; California elected its first Latina governor, Xavier Becerra; and Wisconsin elected its first black governor, David Crowley. I could keep going…

Vogue: Are there any other surprises demographically speaking?

The scale was really big. Look at James Talarico’s margin in Texas. I don’t know how many people here remember the last time we had a Democrat in Texas. It was over 30 years ago. Democrats did not expect to win that big. Expected or not, we have to move forward. How should fashion navigate the new and changing landscape? I think we have to wait and see. Tariffs affected this business in such a major way. The president can still levy tariffs. That does not need congressional approval, as he’s shown us. But I think there’s much more oversight now — for good and bad. I think there will be a lot of hearings, a lot of questions asked. I think that consumers are cautious, more optimistic, and a little calmer. They’re excited, but I think it’s a wait-and-see approach. We have to see how the holidays play out and what this Democratic Congress sets on the agenda in January.

Vogue: All right, let’s talk just a little bit more about the consumer, if we can. What does the election tell us? What message seems to resonate the most?

Affordability. This election, number one: it was against Trump. Number two: it was about affordability. That was the point. He promised to make everything better, and people looked in their bank accounts, their pockets, and at the pump, and everything was worse. I think that affordability will not change. The market is going up, but for those people who are not in the market, their prospects continue to feel scary, so I think people are cautiously optimistic and anxious about prices. So brands need to do even more to reach their consumer directly and show their value to them.

Vogue: There’s a bit of confusion because going into the election consumer sentiment was bad, but yet the economy and spending are up. How can the business people in the room make sense of that?

I think [consumer] sentiment is the median household’s mood. Spending is still [dominated by] the top fifth. Fashion lives in both [percentiles]. Consumer sentiment is near record lows. But North America is still a bright spot. We saw Hermes up 8.8% last quarter. Zegna up 14%. Regardless of the election, we’re making the US a prime market for France for next year.

Vogue: What do you think the knock-on effects of this election will be globally? There’s a lot of big elections coming up in 2027.

Oh yeah, a lot. I think allies are exhaling. I think they’re happy that the American people put a check on this president. I think they’re hoping for a little bit more stability in the last two years of his term. Obviously, the president can do what he wants in Iran, so I think that’s still going to be a major point of instability, especially as it relates to gas prices. But overall, I think sentiment abroad is good.

Vogue: The current administration shaped American industry, particularly in fashion and beauty. How do you think it has impacted it, and how is what happened yesterday likely to change it?

I think it’s going to be about tariffs and production here in the US. This election there were AI [regulatory] measures on the ballot in many, many states, and they [were widely passed]. Many governors who were elected all want an AI data-center moratorium. So I think we will see, at least on the statewide level, a slowdown there. Will that mean a slowdown in manufacturing? Will that have knock-on effects in other industries? It’s a little early to say. It already has a big impact on fashion and beauty businesses. It’ll be interesting to see if that speeds up or slows down. And if there's some more stability in the market, and how businesses react. Are they going to continue to invest in the US as we’ve been hearing ahead of the election, or are they going to pull back? How are we going to invest in retail? I think there’s a lot that we just don’t know yet.

Vogue: How do the results impact how you go about your job at the CFDA, and how do you think it'll impact designers?

We would tell designers that there’s so many programs at the CFDA that we continue to do throughout the year, whether it’s upskilling, whether it’s AI partnerships — trying to help businesses meet the moment they’re in. What we’ve been talking about over the last couple days after the awards and in the lead up to the election is keeping your head down and doing business. Keep talking to your customer. I think this election showed it is a local conversation as much as it is a national one. Don’t let the noise affect you. Just keep your eye on your customer and on your creative work.

Vogue: What does [the results] mean for 2028? I know that the conversations are already starting.

Many people will say the 2028 election starts today. There are two things about 2028 that are interesting. One is what does this do for the Republican party? I think we are not talking about that as much, and we should. Are they the party of Trump anymore? What kind of hold does MAGA have on the future of the party? What does this say about Vice President [JD] Vance and his chances for 2028? I think he was hoping to be the heir apparent. If this is a repudiation of his administration and his policies, what does he have to do?

On the other side, Hakeem Jeffries has a bigger margin than he probably thought he would. There have been a lot of conversations about the new progressive members who are coming. But for candidates in 2028, the biggest thing Democrats have to do is show that they are making Americans’ lives better. That is it. They have to show up for the American people and make their lives better. Otherwise, they’ve done nothing.

Swatch CEO Nick Hayek Addresses That Crazy Royal Pop Rollout

Ranbir Kapoor and Yash’s ‘Ramayana’ Makers on Retelling India’s Epic for the World, as Amazon Music and T-Series Bet on A.R. Rahman and Hans Zimmer’s Soundtrack

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

As “Ramayana Part 1” opens worldwide on Nov. 5, producer Namit Malhotra says the decision to adapt the Indian epic at this scale came once the talent, experience and filmmaking muscle needed to do the story justice were finally in place.

Directed by Nitesh Tiwari (“Dangal,” “Chhichhore”), the first of two films stars Ranbir Kapoor as Rama, the prince sent into exile whose wife Sita (Sai Pallavi) is abducted by Ravana (Yash), the king of Lanka. The score unites A.R. Rahman and Hans Zimmer on an Indian film for the first time. T-Series is the music label, and Amazon Music is the music streaming partner. Related Stories Benjamín Vicuña, Star of Netflix Hit Series 'Envious,' Joins ‘My Last Stand' by Goya-Winner Matías Bize

“We have contributed to some of the biggest films in the world, and I felt, why shouldn’t we bring that same ambition to ‘Ramayana’? Of course, that comes with a tremendous responsibility because of what Lord Ram means to so many people,” Malhotra tells Variety.

Malhotra is the founder and CEO of DNEG, the visual effects company that has won eight Oscars, most recently for “Dune: Part Two.” DNEG and its sister company ReDefine are handling the visual effects on “Ramayana,” which Malhotra’s Prime Focus Studios produces with Yash’s Monster Mind Creations.

For the producer, any global reach has to start with staying faithful to the source. When he first met Zimmer, the composer’s immediate interest was in why the “Ramayana” still resonates with people thousands of years on. “That connection is what we have to honor. We have artists from across the world bringing their best to it, with the hope that Indian audiences feel proud and audiences discovering ‘Ramayana’ for the first time understand why it is so special to us,” Malhotra says.

Tiwari says the opportunity was to bring viewers closer to a story they already love, through intimate performances as well as cinematic scale. “There is an enormous difference between knowing that something happened and feeling what it meant to the people involved. That is where my attention has been: the relationships, the difficult choices and the emotions within those moments,” he says. “Namit’s vision has given us the means to realize this world on an extraordinary scale. My responsibility is to make sure audiences feel connected to the people who inhabit it.”

The characters and major events of the “Ramayana” are familiar to millions, which leaves the director with little room for plot surprises.

“Knowing the outcome doesn’t necessarily lessen the emotion. We return to stories we love because they continue to make us feel something, and sometimes our understanding of them changes as we grow older,” Tiwari says. “The discovery can lie in a character’s reaction, an exchange between two people or the weight of a decision. If you are fully invested in that moment, knowing what comes next can actually make it more moving. My job is to make those moments feel immediate and truthful, so the audience experiences the journey rather than simply recognizes the events.”

His earlier films, he says, showed him how hard audiences respond to the truth of a relationship – a small gesture between a parent and child, or a moment between friends. “The scale is vastly different, and the cultural responsibility is considerable, but I still have to ask whether a scene feels honest and whether we care about the people in it,” Tiwari says. “However ambitious the filmmaking becomes, you cannot lose your attention to those details. They are what give the larger moments their emotional weight.”

Music was central to Malhotra’s plan from day one, and it is also a personal passion. “When I thought about who could bring that vision to life, it was A.R. Rahman and Hans Zimmer. There was no plan B,” he says. “What excited us was the possibility of creating a new sound together. I would tell A.R. Rahman that I wanted the emotional familiarity of the music we love, expressed in a melody of today.”

The score was recorded with an orchestra at AIR Studios in London, where Malhotra watched Indian and Western musicians work on the story together and wished everyone could share the experience. “Hearing their compositions come alive with the orchestra in London was extraordinary. It made me feel that the music could carry the emotion of ‘Ramayana’ to people everywhere,” he says.

From the start, the producer saw music as one of the strongest ways to connect audiences with the film before they reached a cinema, and the first job was writing songs people would want to come back to. Each track has been given its own release window, starting with “Jai Jai Ram,” which arrived on Sept. 14 to coincide with the Ganesh Chaturthi festival. Seeing audiences embrace it was deeply rewarding for Malhotra. “I called A.R. Rahman and said, you have had so many songs that people love, but this is my first with you,” he recalls.

The ambition, he says, is for the songs, performances and events around the album to reach people worldwide and become memorable experiences in their own right. “Whether something becomes a cultural moment is ultimately for people to decide. If these songs become part of their celebrations, their prayers or their everyday lives, that would mean an enormous amount to me.”

Tiwari says he always comes back to what a scene needs – what the character is feeling and what the audience should take from that moment – which decides where music belongs and how much room it gets. “With A.R. Rahman and Hans Zimmer, the conversations are guided by that emotional purpose. There are moments that invite a powerful musical expression and others where a restrained passage or silence can be more affecting,” he says. “For me, the balance is right when the music draws you further into the scene without making you conscious of how it is doing so.”

The director also has a say in how the songs are introduced, working alongside the producers and the music and marketing teams. His focus is on what each song reveals, which visuals frame it and what stays reserved for the cinema. “A song may be someone’s first introduction to our film, so it should communicate its emotion and spirit honestly,” Tiwari says. “At the same time, we want audiences to discover more when they encounter it within the story. Ideally, the release creates a connection, and the film gives that connection greater meaning.”

Looking ahead to the second installment, Malhotra says the first film has to give audiences an experience that stays with them, with their investment in the characters the strongest reason to return. “Music can carry that emotional connection between the two films. A song or a few notes of a theme can bring you straight back to a character, a feeling or a moment,” he says. “My hope is that people keep listening and that the music becomes part of their lives in that interval. Then, when they come back for the second film, they are returning to a world they already feel connected to.”

The soundtrack also sits at the heart of Amazon Music’s push into Indian film music. Indian-language music accounts for around 70% of what the service’s customers in India stream, and for more than two years the company has been building standalone launches for major film albums, both live and on the platform. It brought Hanumankind, Madhubanti Bagchi and Jasmine Sandlas together on one stage in Mumbai for “Dhurandhar,” whose songs then stayed in its 50 Most Played chart long after. It launched the “Border 2” album at the INS Kadamba naval base in Karwar, and staged similar events for “Param Sundari” and “Tere Ishk Mein.”

“Amazon Music is becoming the destination where India’s biggest film albums break,” says Rishabh Gupta, general manager of Amazon Music India. “Streaming services are becoming collaborators in culture, not just a place where music rests, and ‘Ramayana’ is the biggest example of that yet.”

For “Ramayana,” the streamer hosted an Oct. 21 celebration at the Chennai Trade Centre where Rahman performed songs from the film live, joined onstage by Kapoor, Pallavi and Yash. It is also rolling out a short-form series on Instagram and YouTube, including “Decoding Ramayana with A.R. Rahman,” and has built a dedicated destination for the album on the service, along with four new playlists named for characters from the epic. Because Amazon Music operates in more than 50 countries, each song goes live abroad at the same moment it does in India.

Gupta says listening data helps show which songs are landing and with whom, though decisions on what to release and when stay with the filmmakers and T-Series. “In many ways, the music is the film’s first trailer, and a song people love is the best invitation to the theater,” he says.

T-Series’ Bhushan Kumar describes the album as more than a conventional film soundtrack, one that carries the legacy of one of India’s most revered epics to audiences around the world. “With A.R. Rahman and Hans Zimmer coming together, we endeavor to honor its Indian roots while creating a distinctive musical experience that resonates with contemporary and international audiences,” he says. “Our partnership with Amazon Music further strengthens this vision by creating opportunities for focused discovery and connecting the soundtrack with listeners across markets. Ultimately, our aspiration is for the music to transcend the film and become part of a lasting cultural and emotional experience for generations to come.”

Amazon Music gauges a partnership by engagement over time rather than by launch week – whether listeners keep returning after release, and whether the album leads them to music they hadn’t played before. “For ‘Ramayana,’ the real success will be when the songs are still being played long after the film leaves theaters – at festivals, in family routines, and as a way into Indian music for listeners around the world,” Gupta says.

Texas’ New Attorney General Nathan Johnson Is a Former ‘Dragon Ball Z’ Composer

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Johnson composed the musical scores for 67 episodes and five movies.

The Blue Wave that swept through Texas this week also has some Dragon Ball Z energy.

Nathan Johnson, a veteran litigator and Texas state senator, was elected this week as attorney general of the Lone Star State, assuming the role in January 2027.

Johnson lists swimming, reading “boring books,” and making peanut butter as his guilty pleasures, but his interest in the popular anime television series Dragon Ball Z became an early career move. Explore Billboard See latest videos, charts and news

“If you grew up watching Dragon Ball Z, you might know me better than you think: I composed the musical scores for 67 episodes and five movies, and had a great time doing it,” he writes on his Facebook page. Related Hitsujibungaku, Kyary Pamyu Pamyu & Otoboke Beaver to Play Japanese Music Showcase matsuri '26 in London M!LK Earns Second Japan Hot 100 No. 1 With 'That's! Chojo' as Vaundy's 'Shiwaawase' Reaches Top 3 Tinashe Sends Message to Republican Fans During 2026 Midterm Elections: 'Y'all Can Go'

Those “wall to wall” scores were cut in the 2000s and include Dragon Ball Z: The World’s Strongest, Dragon Ball Z: The Tree of Might, Dragon Ball Z: Broly — Second Coming, Dragon Ball Z: Fusion Reborn and Dragon Ball Z: Wrath of the Dragon.

“It was a lot of fun,” Johnson recounts in a video posted in June. “I had a one-year-old at the time, and I remember him sitting on my knee and stuff blowing up. He was like ‘oh, daddy, Goku,’” he continued, identifying the show’s lead character.

“He just thought it was super cool. Even then it just gave me street cred with my kids’ friends and it gave me a creative outlet and a cultural connection that very few people had the opportunity to have.”

Created by Akira Toriyama and produced by Toei Animation, Dragon Ball Z is a sci-fi fantasy that follows the adventures of Goku, an alien warrior. The anime premiered in 1989 in Japan and was syndicated in the United States on Cartoon Network from 1998 until 2008. Its celebrity fans include Kendrick Lamar, Steve Aoki, Bad Bunny, Big Sean and Anuel AA.

The multihyphenate Johnson holds a B.S. in physics from the University of Arizona and a law degree from the University of Texas at Austin, and took office in 2019. Since then, he has authored at least 433 bills and more than 130 into law.

Johnson won the Texas attorney general election on Wednesday, Nov. 3 as the Democrats routed the Republicans in the midterms, seizing control of the Senate and the House of Representatives. On the way to an historic victory, the Dems flipped seven Senate seats, nearly 30 House districts, five governorships and up to 12 state legislative chambers.

Nikki Minaj is Going Down With Trump’s Ship After Nearly Every Candidate She Backed Lost

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

After Democrats flipped both the House and the Senate, Donald Trump had a full-throttle meltdown — and Nicki Minaj followed suit.

The rapper spent much of the last several months as one of MAGA’s favorite mouthpieces, popping up at various White House events and even hitting the campaign trail alongside Trump in an attempt to boost Republicans in the midterm elections. Now that Democrats won just about everything in a nationwide rebuke of Trump and the Republican Party, Minaj is doubling down and planting her feet firm on the bandwagon she tossed herself on earlier this year.

After a brief period of silence following the midterm results, Minaj aired out her grievances the day after with a 15-point post on X. Among the items on her listicle, the rapper made a range of statements from the obvious, “Trump is STILL your PRESIDENT,” to the not-so-obvious, “Maybe now our people will WAKE UP.” Minaj also congratulated Republican Congresswoman Anna Paulina Luna for winning Florida’s 13th Congressional District race (although the Associated Press had yet to call it at time of publication) and staunch Trump supporter Byron Donalds, who won the state’s governor’s race.

Notably, as fact-checked by Snopes, most of the candidates that Minaj publicly endorsed during the midterm elections lost including Republican Mike Rogers of Michigan, who lost to Democrat Abdul El-Sayed; Republican candidate for New York governor, Bruce Blakeman, who lost to incumbent Gov. Kathy Hochul; and Republican gubernatorial candidate John James, who lost to Democrat Jocelyn Benson in Michigan governor’s race.

Donalds appears to be the one successful candidate out of Minaj’s picks.

Elsewhere on X, Minaj took a swipe at the masses and wrote, “Elon is STILL a trillionaire and you are STILL posting on his app. I’m sure the shareholders appreciate the dialogue.” She also gave Musk’s Grok chatbot a shoutout and called it a “phenomenal product that has significantly changed my life for the better.”

The bizarre ramble is the latest in Minaj’s slump into Trump’s MAGA universe. In October, Minaj hopped on Air Force One with Trump on their way to a rally and held a mock interview with the president. She lauded Trump as a businessman and a “great guy” who had “won three elections,” though he has won two. Steam Makes Brand-New RPG Free To Keep Forever

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Steam is always getting free games, but the quality of those free titles can sometimes be hit or miss. While countless games have gotten free demos or free early access periods, the window is a bit smaller when it comes to games that are 100% free to keep forever. Thankfully, there's a brand-new RPG that's on Steam now, and not only is it free to keep, it's actually pretty solid, currently sitting with "Positive" reviews after being available for only the last 24 hours.

Arguably, most free RPGs on Steam are actually free-to-play and gacha games that will entice players with a purchase or two along the way. That's thankfully not the case here. Instead, you're being offered a completely free RPG that requires nothing but your time. It's admittedly pretty short (most people can beat it in around 2 hours), but for an RPG that's being offered up at no cost, it's a pretty sweet deal.

WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones is a brand-new RPG on Steam that pays homage to the classic JRPGs. It has classic pixel-art, giving it a SNES look that should remind fans of games from that era like Final Fantasy 6, Chrono Trigger, and Secret of Mana. You'll also need to solve puzzles, giving it some serious The Legend of Zelda vibes, and fight off against mech bosses.

As you might expect, you'll play as Wendy herself, but there are a number of party members joining you along the way. With enemies like Golden Archers and Hot Sludge Sundaes, the Dragon Quest-like puns should keep you smiling the whole way through. Yes, this is all just a clever and shameless marketing scheme to get us all to buy food from Wendy's, but at least it's a GOOD marketing scheme.

WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones is available to play on Steam Deck as well, so you can chip away while on the go (maybe even while waiting in a drive-thru). The RPG is also 100% free on Steam, and it's yours to keep. So, even if you're just morbidly curious to see what a fast food-themed JRPG would be like, you can check it out at zero risk and zero cost. The game currently sits with "Positive" review scores on Steam.

Wendy's has been on an absolute tear lately when it comes to clever marketing. Songs to Listen to in a Wendy's Parking Lot, a 6-track emo EP, came out a month ago. It was surprisingly catchy and well-done, and anyone who grew up in the early 2000s should instantly feel nostalgic. Now, that's being followed up with the RPG, WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones. At this point, I'm just excited to see what the company comes up with next.

It's no secret the fast food chain has been suffering when it comes to sales lately, but this kind of clever marketing is surely a way to keep Wendy's at the front of people's minds. Be sure to check out WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones on Steam. In terms of free RPGs, it's actually a lot of fun.

I had officially closed the book on volumizing hot brushes. Like half the internet, I fell victim to the Revlon One-Step craze a few years back. While it certainly had the smoothing factor down, it felt like it was actively ripping chunks of hair from my scalp with every pass.

My Dyson Airwrap attachment was undeniably gentler, sure, but it never quite delivered the rich, bouncy volume I was actually chasing. Eventually, I resigned myself to heatless curl rods to fake the blowout aesthetic — only to spend the rest of the day mourning the catastrophic amount of curl shrinkage.

For someone prone to breakage, the difference in hand-feel was immediate. My routine was simple. I detangled thoroughly, split my hair into two manageable tiers (top and bottom), and worked in generous sections.

Once the baseline was uniform, I went back through the ends to roll and bevel them under for that bouncy, layered finish.

Finding tools that respect coarse, curly, and fragile hair without requiring professional-level arm acrobatics is rare. The $1.4 Billion ‘No-Brainer’ Opportunity The Music Industry Is Missing

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Soundtrack, the music streamer used by restaurants and retailers globally, estimates billions are lost in revenue every year in the U.S. from businesses that unwittingly stream music illegally.

By Elizabeth Dilts Marshall

While music industry leaders discuss superfan monetization and craft economic models for AI music applications that don’t yet exist, Ola Sars, founder and head of Soundtrack, says they are missing out on $1.4 billion in royalties that go uncollected in the United States every year from businesses illegally playing copyrighted music.

That’s according to a new study released Thursday (Nov. 5) by Soundtrack, a commercial music streaming platform backed by Spotify that is now used by 60,000 businesses in 75 countries. Related Kenny MacPherson, Veteran Music Publisher and Big Deal Music Co-Founder, Dies at 71 Jessica Lynch

The independent study, which Soundtrack commissioned, surveyed around 1,500 representatives of some 15,000 U.S. business locations and found that only 19% of those that play music are fully compliant with commercial music requirements. Nearly 80% of U.S. businesses use consumer streaming services, which are only licensed for personal use, to play music in-store, and just 5% reported having licenses from all four major performing rights organizations (PROs): ASCAP, BMI, GMR and SESAC.

At a time when the revenue growth from subscription and ad-supported streaming is slowing, Sars says the music industry could “unlock exponential growth” in the money it collects from U.S. businesses by turning their attention to this “extreme” level of non-compliance.

“People are playing music already, but they simply don’t know that it’s not licensed for commercial use. They haven’t read the terms and no one has told them,” Sars tells Billboard. “It is what you guys in the U.S. would call a pretty big no-brainer. If you’re playing music in your business, artists should be fairly compensated. There is a rate structure in place, and you can’t use a personal service in your business. But it’s complicated.”

Commercial music streaming in businesses can be the next growth area in music, Sars says. Soundtrack says it recently partnered with SESAC to round out its pitch to U.S. businesses.

Based in Sweden, Soundtrack generates roughly 60 million euros ($67 million) in annual recurring revenue and is on track to generate $100 million by 2028. Previously called Soundtrack Your Brand, the company raised $15 million in 2023 from investors like Matt Pincus’ MUSIC and Dundee Partners, who joined existing investors Balderton Capital, Fuel Venture Capital, Industrifonden, Telia, DIG and Matt Spetzler.

“Ultimately, our aim is to create a healthy ecosystem for the background music industry by growing the ‘pie’ for music creators and rights-holders, while not breaking the bank for hard-working businesses,” SARS says.

Adam Driver Says ‘The Ship Has Sailed’ on ‘The Hunt for Ben Solo’ and He’s Done Playing Kylo Ren: ‘It’s Over’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Bad news for “Star Wars” fans: Adam Driver is squashing any possibility of returning as Kylo Ren to the galaxy.

On a recent episode of “The Rich Eisen Show,” Eisen asked Driver if he thought he’d ever play the villain in the franchise again. The “Girls” actor responded: ‘Yeah, it’s over… No more ‘Star Wars.’” We “tried to do one more,” said Driver “but they’re like, ‘No, he’s dead,’” referring to Disney studio executives Bob Iger and Alan Bergman, who rejected a screenplay for a standalone movie centered on Kylo Ren that he’d pitched with Steven Soderbergh. Related Stories Benjamín Vicuña, Star of Netflix Hit Series 'Envious,' Joins ‘My Last Stand' by Goya-Winner Matías Bize

“I always was interested in doing another ‘Star Wars,’” Driver said at the time. “We presented the script to Lucasfilm. They loved the idea. They totally understood our angle and why we were doing it. We took it to Bob Iger and Alan Bergman, and they said no. They didn’t see how Ben Solo was alive. And that was that.”

During the interview, Driver continued, reiterating that Soderbergh was attached to direct, and that “we worked on it for a few years, really and earnestly. We got a script together that was really good with Scott Burns, Rebecca Blunt, and Soderbergh and I. We all worked on it… It was awesome. It was really cool. But you know, I don’t get to decide.”

Eisen then pressed Driver on whether he’d return if someone were to “resurrect” the project. “If they wanted to, fine. But we’re not… It’s not our call,” said Driver. “I think the ship has sailed though.” Driver did acknowledge the “fans response” to the dropped film, which he described as “pretty incredible.” I didn’t expect that it was going to be billboards and people flying planes over Disney and things like that. That was pretty cool.”

Back when the news broke about the film, a group of “Star Wars” fans paid for a plane to fly a banner reading “Save The Hunt for Ben Solo” over Walt Disney Studios in Burbank, California.

In April, Soderbergh shared a similar sentiment to Driver, saying that the film “would have happened,” “if it was gonna happen.”

Netflix's Addictive 2-Season Western Is a Near-Perfect Streaming Binge

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

From Dallas to Yellowstone, soapy Western shows make for some of the best binge-watches. They are messy and entertaining, with intense plotlines that range from land disputes to tangled love triangles. They are the perfect escape to watch when looking for a show to get totally drawn into, because they are pretty easy to follow along with, and there is never a dull moment amongst all the drama.

Netflix's Ransom Canyon is based on the book series by Jodi Thomas, and it's a show perfect for TV fans who like small-town dramas, swoonworthy romances, and shocking plot twists. For those looking for their next binge-watch, Ransom Canyon is the perfect choice, and although the series didn't get more than two seasons, it's still the perfect binge for those looking for a light-hearted Western romance.

Ransom Canyon follows the residents of the small town of Ransom Canyon, Texas, most of whom have lived there for their whole lives. At the center of it all is Staten Kirkland (Josh Duhamel), a wealthy ranch owner who's been struggling after two major personal tragedies. Two years after the death of his wife, Staten had to deal with the sudden death of his teenage son, Randall (Hubert Smielecki), in a car accident. Now, a year after Randall's death, Staten tries to move forward with his life despite pressure from his wife's brother, Davis Collins (Eoin Macken), to sell some of his land so that a new pipeline can be built in its place. Staten also finds himself entangled in a messy love triangle. Just as his feelings for his old friend, Quinn O'Grady (Minka Kelly), start to resurface, Davis starts pursuing a romance with her as well.

Four icons and four completely different ways of walking into trouble. This is the one you would have been out there, why it fits, and what it would have cost you.

You have never explained yourself and you have never needed to, because people work out fairly quickly that you are the most capable person in the room without you ever having to mention it.

You are the funniest person in most rooms and the most loyal person in all of them, and you would be genuinely insulted if somebody suggested those two things were in any tension.

You never wanted this job, and you took it anyway because the alternative was standing there watching somebody less careful do it badly, which is the most Western reason there has ever been.

Nobody has ever successfully talked you out of something you had already decided was right, and a long line of grown adults has found that out the hard way.

The messy web of Ransom Canyon goes way beyond just Staten's intense feud with Davis, though. Newcomer Yancy Grey (Jack Schumacher) comes to Ransom Canyon to work for Cap Fuller (James Brolin), another ranch owner whose land Davis is eyeing for the pipeline. At first glance, Yancy looks like a hard worker who cares about his job and who's quickly falling for Quinn's employee and friend, Ellie Estevez (Marianly Tejada). Yancy has quite a few secrets, though, and his motivations are much more complex than they seem to be at first — as are his connections to some of Ransom's longtime residents. Yancy is not just there to work on Cap's ranch by chance, which Ellie figures out quickly, and she has to reconcile her growing feelings for him with the clear mountain of secrets that he is keeping from her.

In the wake of Randall's death, his cousin and Davis' son, Reid (Andrew Liner), is spiraling. Little does he know, his ex-girlfriend, Lauren (Lizzy Greene), is secretly dating his best friend, Lucas (Garrett Wareing). That's nothing compared to the pressure that Davis puts on him to succeed, especially now. Davis' interest in the pipeline isn't coming out of nowhere; if he can't make this deal work, he could lose the ranch that has been in his family for generations, all because of his own father's carelessness with his finances. Everyone in Ransom Canyon has a lot to either gain or lose from the pipeline deal, which makes this storyline feel particularly high-stakes, especially on top of everything else.

Ransom Canyon is an addictive show to watch, and once you start the first episode, it's easy to quickly binge-watch all 18 at once. In addition to the romantic drama and tension related to the pipeline, Ransom Canyon is also a murder mystery. Staten's wife died of illness, but Randall's death is more complicated. It was initially believed that Randall's car was the only one on the road that night, and that the accident was a terrible but random tragedy. At the start of the series, though, Staten starts finding clues that lead him to believe that somebody else caused the accident, whether intentionally or not. He pushes the sheriff, Lauren's father, Dan Brigman (Philip Winchester), to investigate, but the sheriff believes that Staten is just grieving, so he initially has to look into it on his own.

Essential Western television for lovers of "simpler" times.

With a cozy small-town setting and soapy romantic and business drama, Ransom Canyon is a fun and messy watch that's perfect for those looking for an easy watch. It is packed with compelling conflict, primarily related to the pipeline, as well as some twists and turns about the characters and their histories with each other. Most of all, though, the series is about Staten and Quinn's journeys. After losing both his wife and his son, Staten is isolated and angry in his grief, and the show's first season shows him slowly learning how to open up and let himself care about other people again.

Meanwhile, Quinn has always felt overlooked, especially after having to return home from her time away focusing on her music. Now, she gets the chance to make the dance hall she owns into something really special, but the cost of getting the money she needs could mean supporting the pipeline and everything it entails. Staten and Quinn have also always had unresolved feelings for each other, even after he married her best friend, so they now find those feelings coming up again even as they try to bury them.

Ransom Canyon is available to stream on Netflix.

Ransom Canyon is a drama set in a small Texas town, where tensions escalate as three ranching dynasties vie for land, legacy, and love.

The Best Fantasy Book You Haven't Read Hits Harder Today Than Ever Before

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

We live in a complex time, entertainment-wise. AI is a genuine risk to art forms of all types, and large businesses are taking over each other, acting suspiciously like a monopoly. That's without mentioning how streaming keeps changing the name of the game for everyone and everything, and we keep finding entertainment in efforts that appeal to the lowest common denominator.

The writing was on the wall for years, and many visionary authors predicted the future of the entertainment industry with such accuracy that it's outright frightening. Then there are those who took it to the extreme, satirizing the very concept of what we find entertaining and offering a scathing view of it: that's exactly what Matthew Woodring Stover did in his underrated 1997 gem, Heroes Die. Set in a bleak version of the future, this science-fantasy tale features a wildly creative and unforgiving critique of corporate media and the audience's obsession with spectacle that makes it all the more powerful today.

Heroes Die is set in a dystopian, 23rd-century Earth where society is organized in strict castes and corporations run things following a plague outbreak that collapsed the previous governments. Coexisting with reality is Overworld, a parallel dimension of fantastical creatures and magic, where warring empires wreak havoc. Corporations have found a way to sell experiences to people on Earth by hiring actors from Earth to assume personas on Overworld and go on real, high-stakes adventures, which are then sold to the audience as an immersive, first-person experience.

The plot centers on Hari Michaelson, an actor who assumes the ruthless identity of Caine in Overworld, known as the Blade of Tyshalle across the city of Ankhana. When his estranged wife, Pallas Ri, is captured in Ankhana and her connection to Earth is severed, Hari agrees to go into Overworld as Caine to rescue her. However, he must also kill the ruling emperor per the request of the studio executives, as the ongoing succession crisis and potential civil war would make for a great setting for future adventures.

The world of Heroes Die is ruthless and bleak, grimdark literature at its finest. This version of Earth is pure nightmare fuel, with corporations controlling nearly every aspect of culture, and therefore everyday life. Overworld isn't much better, as the violent, survival-of-the-fittest credo makes for a dangerous and perennially brutal way of life. Stover perfectly blends genres, embracing all the aesthetics and themes of fantasy and updating them through a distinct sci-fi lens. Indeed, the book exists right in the middle, never giving in to one or the other.

From the very beginning, the world of Heroes Die feels like an infinitely darker and exaggerated yet uncomfortably familiar version of our current situation. Sure, we don't have a fantasy alternate reality coexisting with ours (that we know of), but thematically and in everything that concerns Earth, this dystopia feels dangerously possible. For starters, there's the mysterious plague that collapsed the previous structures, which in a post-COVID world feels not only feasible but perhaps inevitable. Then, there's the fact that corporations have taken over and dictate society's way of life, shaping it through the ruthless entertainment system they pioneered and control. Movies like Network have also toyed with this idea — that corporations are our new religion and can easily manipulate us — but Heroes Die makes it feel all the more real because it makes the entertainment so enticing.

Indeed, the book makes us complicit in the violence it's critiquing. Stover doesn't pull back with the guts and gore: Heroes Die features extreme sequences of intense and unforgiving violence, some of which verge on the cruel and are actively tough to read through. Yet, we remain hooked to the page, not only because of his gift as a wordsmith, but because the world he created is as engaging and alluring as it is repulsive. Think of this book as a literary version of Michael Haneke's Funny Games: it's openly judging you for partaking in the violence it's depicting, even daring, wanting you to turn away, but you just... don't. Our collective need for sensationalism is crucial here, and Stover understands and weaponizes it.

Here is where the book's takedown of modern entertainment values rings the strongest. Heroes Die is a scathing denunciation of what we find entertaining and where we place value. It not only predicted the rise of reality television as a dominant cultural force, but it also accurately guessed that someone, somewhere, at some point in time, would find a way to sell violence as amusement. Like some of its spiritual siblings, including Stephen King's The Running Man, Heroes Die sadly acknowledges that we find degradation fun, especially if we find a way to justify our enjoyment. "It's all staged" and "none of it is true" are constant reminders that real people are engaging in those acts, whether by greed, need, or both.

Books like Heroes Die take a mundane concept (a man willing to rescue his wife while dealing with corporate interests) and introduce an out-of-this-world concept to make it more engaging. Yet, the struggle is still familiar and, to many, relatable. At its core, the book is about what we're willing to do for someone we care about, but it's also about how we survive in a world dictated by profit and loss, and what we're willing to sacrifice to do it. In a way, it says that violence is not only inevitable, but also an inherent part of us, to the point where we can't even be rid of it by escaping into a fantasy world of gods and emperors. In fact, it's so natural to us that we're not only prone to it, but also compelled by it. Harrowingly, violence here is both the question and the answer, the crime and the punishment. By merely existing, Heroes Die is proving its point.

North Korean Ad Fraudsters Pwn Binance; Don’t Bother Predicting Your Ad Revenue

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The ad industry has a long history of costly blunders – some absurd, some sinister – that have run into the millions.

Remember the night of the yellow square? Or, more troublingly, a recent rash of Google ad account takeovers?

If you hadn’t heard, Binance was totally pwned in a $6 billion crypto heist last week, suspected to be a North Korean operation. Well, Binance has now published what amounts to a preliminary autopsy report of the incident.

The heist began when a Binance Wallet DevOps team member accidentally downloaded a malicious copy of Homebrew – a popular open-source tool – from a mirror site promoted in a search ad. The malware was spotted, but not before the hackers had obtained the real identities of Wallet DevOps team members.

Using those identities, the North Korean hacking unit spent months targeting Binance’s wallet team with covert schemes. One involved a prolonged fake job interview that lured a Binance DevOps engineer into downloading an “assignment” onto their work device.

After taking control of the Binance DevOps engineer’s laptop, the hacker used an unnamed internal AI tool to log in to the devices of other engineers. Access to multiple credentials allowed the heist to go through.

A $6 billion reminder that one ad click can come at a staggering cost.

Publishers are contending with AI search eating into their traffic. But at The New York Times, the problem for investors wasn’t shrinking ad revenue; it was that growth isn’t happening fast enough for their liking.

The company reported on Wednesday that its Q3 digital ad revenue grew 12% to $109.9 million. Good stuff. But it slightly missed its own projection of growth in the “mid to high teens.”

Most publishers would kill for 12% growth right now. But it was the slowest digital ad growth rate the Times has posted since Q4 2024, when it grew by 9.5%.

Multiple investors pressed Times CEO Meredith Kopit Levien on the ad revenue miss. But, although she acknowledged in her opening remarks that the Times “isn’t immune” to “Big Tech companies whose moves continue to result in less traffic to publishers,” she didn’t directly answer questions about whether AI search contributed to the revenue shortfall. Instead, she kept reiterating that it’s hard to predict ad revenue quarter to quarter.

The NYT’s reluctance to directly address AI search contrasts with publishers like People Inc. and platforms like Reddit, which have been openly blasting Google for obliterating their search traffic.

Meanwhile, News Corp also reported earnings on Wednesday. The company’s Dow Jones division – which includes The Wall Street Journal, Barron’s, MarketWatch and more – also grew digital ad revenue by 12% last quarter. No investors pooh-pooh’d News Corp’s growth, though. One even offered his congratulations.

Guess that’s a lesson for publishers: These days, predicting your ad revenue may just be a liability.

Does opting out of being tracked by your smart TV actually work? According to new research from Northeastern University, not always, The Verge reports.

As part of the study, researchers analyzed network traffic from 12 different TV devices running eight operating systems, which included big players like LG, Amazon Fire, Google TV, Samsung, Roku, Vizio and VIDAA OS. They then checked which TVs had automatic content recognition (ACR) enabled by default and whether changing the sharing settings stopped those TVs from sending data.

Most TVs tracked viewing on their own built-in streaming services, but not on Netflix or YouTube. More than half also tracked content coming in through HDMI, such as from a cable box or game console. Opting out stopped the data flow on most devices – but Samsung and Roku TVs kept sending data from their own streaming services, while Walmart-owned Vizio TVs continued transmitting as before.

ACR is now table stakes for the smart TV industry, in part because it helps advertisers measure ad reach and frequency. But it’s also drawing more scrutiny – and opprobrium – from viewers who are only now realizing just how much of their viewing gets tracked.

The bots aren’t taking over just yet. Uber says it only fulfilled four rides and 500 food orders from third-party AI agents last quarter out of 320 million transactions. [The Verge]

Crypto PACs spent $170 million on the midterms and have very little to show for it. [Fortune]

The same goes for the $440 million that Elon Musk personally poured into election ads across the country. [WSJ]

Another big loser in the midterms: AI data centers. All thirteen municipalities that had proposals on the ballot to restrict data center construction appear to have passed those proposals. [Forbes] Also, the most vocally anti-data center Senate candidates won their races. [Politico]

Meanwhile, Super PACs that aimed to promote AI-friendly politicians barely mentioned AI in their ads, preferring to highlight other issues instead. [Bellingcat]

Titan OS raised $46 million in seed funding, much of which could go toward expanding its ad business on smart TVs. [TechCrunch]

Understanding AI’s carbon impact and how to make more sustainable choices when using generative AI. [report]
